Shuffle algebras, lattice paths and quantum toroidal 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n | m \mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
Alexandr Garbali and Andrei Negu t ,

Abstract
. We describe and compute various families of commuting elements of the matrix shuffle algebra (​​ [ 19 ] ) of type 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n | m \mathfrak{gl}_{n|m} , which is expected to be isomorphic to quantum toroidal 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n | m \mathfrak{gl}_{n|m} (​​ [ 2 , 18 ] ). Our formulas are given in terms of partial traces of products of R R -matrices of the quantum affine algebra U t ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 ˙ n | m ) U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m}) , and have a lattice path interpretation following [ 13 ] . Our calculations are based on the machinery of the quantum toroidal algebras and a new anti-homomorphism between matrix shuffle algebras.



1.
Introduction

Shuffle algebras associated with quantum toroidal algebras have been an active topic of research in the past years. They appear in the construction of quantum toroidal algebras and in their geometric representations
[
10
,
12
,
22
,
17
]
, calculations of integrals of motion
[
9
,
5
,
7
,
8
]
and the Bethe ansatz
[
15
,
6
]
.

The quantum toroidal algebra of type
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
has two (in a sense orthogonal) realizations as double shuffle algebras: the first of these, denoted by
\mathcal{S}
, is more standard and traces its origin to
[
9
]
and
[
4
]
; we recall it in Subsection
2.5
. The second realization, denoted by
\mathcal{A}
, was introduced in
[
19
]
and has been less studied; it is a vector space of rational functions with matrix valued coefficients living in
\text{End}(V\otimes\cdots\otimes V)
with
V\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}
. For this reason we refer to
\mathcal{A}
as the
matrix
shuffle algebra and by this we distinguish it from
\mathcal{S}

1
1

1

For
n=1
the two algebras
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}
coincide.
. Such shuffle algebra presentations of quantum toroidal algebras offer a number of technical advantages. Instead of having abstract generators, relations and multiplication rules, in the shuffle presentation one has explicit rational functions and an explicit multiplication, called the
shuffle multiplication
. A notable example of the advantage offered by the shuffle algebra presentation is given by a description of commutative subalgebras of toroidal algebras
[
5
,
7
,
11
]
. This is best illustrated in the case of the quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
in
[
5
]
, where the authors provided explicit formulas for several families of elements of the commuting shuffle algebra and proved their commutativity with respect to the shuffle multiplication.

There exists an alternative approach to the commutative subalgebra of the
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
shuffle algebra due to
[
14
,
13
]
. In this approach one computes partition functions of ensembles of coloured paths on a finite portion of the square lattice with specific boundary conditions and local Boltzmann weights given by the matrix elements of the
R
-matrix of
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m})
. These partition functions produce families of rational functions which belong to the space of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
commuting shuffle algebra. By tuning the boundary conditions appropriately one can compute different families of shuffle algebra elements, including those of
[
5
]
. In
[
13
]
the boundary conditions are chosen to be “conic”, which means that the lattice partition functions can be written as a partial trace of products of
R
-matrices. Such trace formulas can be manipulated to reproduce the definition of the shuffle product, thus giving a new way of proving shuffle commutativity.

In this paper we generalize the results of
[
13
]
to the case of the commuting subalgebra of the matrix shuffle algebra of type
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
. Our generalization relies on Conjecture
1
, which states that the quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
is isomorphic to the double matrix shuffle algebra. This Conjecture was proved in the case
m=0
in
[
19
]
. A key element in our approach is the construction of explicit anti-homomorphisms (of independent interest) between the matrix shuffle algebra of type
\mathfrak{gl}_{n^{\prime}|m^{\prime}}
and the matrix shuffle algebra of type
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
.

1.1.
Conic partition function

Consider a cone pointing upwards and the square lattice drawn on it as follows. Draw
N
directed parallel lines which wrap around the tip of the cone and then self-intersect:

(1.1)

The points where the lattice intersects the “base” of the cone will be called
boundary points
. On this square lattice we draw coloured paths which are either closed or begin and end on two boundary points. The former will sometimes be called loops and the latter boundary paths. The colours of paths will be labelled by the set
\mathcal{I}=\{1\ldots n+m\}
where the labels
i
have an associated
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading given by
(-1)^{\delta_{i>n}}
. This grading leads to the interpretation of “bosonic” and “fermionic” paths. Drawing a coloured path on this lattice should follow the directions indicated by the arrows in (
1.1
). In the following example we have a configuration with one red loop, one green loop, one green boundary path and one blue boundary path:

\alpha_{4}
\beta_{4}
z_{4}
qz_{4}
\alpha_{3}
\beta_{3}
z_{3}
qz_{3}
\alpha_{2}
\beta_{2}
z_{2}
qz_{2}
\alpha_{1}
\beta_{1}
z_{1}
qz_{1}

(1.2)

What may appear as an intersection of two green lines in the above picture should be interpreted as a touching of a green loop and a green boundary path. We can take the labeling set of the colours to be
\mathcal{I}=\{1,2,3,4\}
with the identification
\{
no path, red, green, blue
\}=\{1,2,3,4\}
. The occupation of the boundary points by paths is recorded by
\alpha=(\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{4})
and
\beta=(\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{4})
, so that we have
\alpha=(1,3,4,1)
and
\beta=(1,1,4,3)
. The parameters
z_{1}\ldots z_{4}
and
qz_{1}\ldots qz_{4}
are called the
spectral parameters
, they are “carried” by the directed lines of the lattice which are directly above them. The winding of a lattice line around the tip of the cone changes the value of the corresponding spectral parameter by a factor of
q
. These parameters will enter the Boltzmann weights as discussed below.

By specifying
n,m
and
N
we can draw all possible path configurations. These configurations can be grouped according to specific occupations of the boundary points given by
\alpha
and
\beta
. Each configuration
C
carries a weight
W_{C}
which is computed by multiplying the values of all local Boltzmann weights
2
2

2

These Boltzmann weights correspond to the coefficients of the
R
-matrix of the algebra
U_{t}(\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m})
computed in
[
1
]
.
:

\scriptstyle x
\scriptstyle y
:

\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle i

\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle j
\scriptstyle j

\scriptstyle j
\scriptstyle j
\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle i

\begin{cases}\dfrac{t^{-1/2}-t^{1/2}x/y}{1-x/y}&i\leqslant n\\
\dfrac{t^{-1/2}x/y-t^{1/2}}{1-x/y}&i>n\end{cases}

\begin{cases}\dfrac{(t^{-1/2}-t^{1/2})x/y}{1-x/y}&i<j\\
\dfrac{(t^{-1/2}-t^{1/2})}{1-x/y}&i>j\end{cases}

1

(1.3)

where red “
i
” and green “
j
” can be replaced by any pair of distinct colours. We attach the label
1\in\mathcal{I}
to the edges which have no path. The vertex with all labels equal to
i
is interpreted as two paths of the same colour
i
touching each other but not intersecting. The weight of this vertex depends on whether the label
i
is bosonic or fermionic which is determined by the gradation. The parameters
x
and
y
are the spectral parameters. These parameters should be replaced by the appropriate
z_{a}
and
qz_{b}
carried by the paths.

For a fixed
N
,
\mathcal{I}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{I}^{N}
, the collection of all global configurations on the
N
by
N
lattice (
1.1
) with such bosonic and fermionic paths is denoted by
\Omega_{\alpha,\beta}
, where the two indices
\alpha
and
\beta
specify the boundary conditions at the base of the cone as explained under (
1.2
). In addition to the local weights (
1.3
) each configuration
C
is multiplied by a factor which accounts for the closed loops content of the configuration, see (
1.4
). The partition function of such lattice paths is defined by:

\displaystyle Z_{\alpha,\beta}=\sum_{C\in\Omega_{\alpha,\beta}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}}u_{i}^{\Lambda_{i}(C)}\times W_{C}

(1.4)

where
\Lambda_{i}(C)
denotes the total number of loops of colour
i
in
C
. Therefore, the new variables
u_{i}
count bosonic loops for
i\leqslant n
and fermionic loops for
i>n
.

Let
V\cong\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
have a standard orthonormal basis with vectors labeled by
\mathcal{I}
. For the basis vectors of
V^{\otimes N}
we use
\alpha\in\mathcal{I}^{N}
as labels. Then
Z_{\alpha,\beta}
with different
\alpha
and
\beta
can be combined to form a matrix
Z_{N}
:

\displaystyle Z_{N}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{I}^{N}}Z_{\alpha,\beta}\left|\alpha\right\rangle\left\langle\beta\right|

(1.5)

Furthermore, the various
Z_{N}
can be collected into a generating function:

\displaystyle Z(v)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}Z_{N}v^{N}

(1.6)

One of the main results of this paper is an expression for
Z(v)
in terms of a ‘‘shuffle exponential’’
3
3

3

In the main text we will write
\exp
for
\exp_{*}
.
:

\displaystyle\exp_{*}(A):=1+A+\frac{1}{2!}A*A+\frac{1}{3!}A*A*A+\cdots

where
*
is the shuffle product of the matrix shuffle algebra associated to the quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
, see (
2.23
).

Theorem 1
.

For any
n,m\geqslant 0
, the generating function
Z(v)
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle Z(v)=\exp_{*}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{v^{k}}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\left(w_{i+1}^{k}-t^{\epsilon_{i}k}w_{i}^{k}\right)S_{k}^{(i)}\right)

(1.7)

where
w_{i}:=t^{-\epsilon_{i}/2}\epsilon_{i}u_{i}
,
u_{n+m+1}:=qu_{1}
,
\epsilon_{i}=(-1)^{\delta_{i>n}}
, and
S_{k}^{(1)}\ldots S_{k}^{(n+m)}\in\emph{End}(V^{\otimes k})(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
is a collection of matrix-valued rational functions which can be computed recursively:

(z_{1}+\dots+z_{k})S_{k}^{(i)}=\left[S_{k-1}^{(i)},z_{1}S_{1}^{(i)}\right],\qquad S_{1}^{(i)}=\frac{-1}{1-q}{\text{diag}}\{\overset{i}{\overbrace{1\ldots 1}},q\ldots q\}

(1.8)

where
[a,b]=a*b-b*a
. Theorem
1
is contingent on Conjecture
1
, which was proved for
m=0
(​​
[
19
]
).

The matrix elements of
S_{k}^{(i)}
themselves can also be written as conic partition functions of the form (
1.4
) where the summation runs over all configurations with boundary paths coloured by
\{1\ldots n+m+2\}\setminus\{i+1,i+2\}
and where the loops are allowed to be only of colours
i+1
(bosonic) and
i+2
(fermionic) and the
u
-factors of (
1.4
) must be replaced with
\Lambda_{i+2}(-1)^{\Lambda_{i+2}}
(see (
1.17
))).

Because we have for all
i,j,k,l
the equality

S_{k}^{(i)}*S_{l}^{(j)}=S_{l}^{(j)}*S_{k}^{(i)}

formula (
1.7
) is unambiguous, and moreover the matrices
S_{k}^{(i)}
can be viewed as elements (in fact generators) of a commuting subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{+}\subset\mathcal{A}^{+}
that we recall in Subsection
4.1
. The matrices
Z_{N}
therefore also represent commuting elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
. Theorem
1
is a generalization of the shuffle exponential formula of
[
13
]
for the
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
shuffle algebra. Indeed, in
[
13
]
the definition of the partition function prevents having boundary paths. Choosing the boundary conditions at the base of the cylinder (
1.1
) to be free of paths corresponds to taking the vacuum-vacuum expectation value of
Z_{N}
:

\left\langle 1^{N}\right|Z_{N}\left|1^{N}\right\rangle

By computing this expectation value of
Z(v)
in (
1.7
) one can reproduce the exponential formula given in Theorem 1.1 of
[
13
]
.

1.2.
An anti-isomorphism of shuffle algebras

Let us explain the underlying algebraic reasons for the existence of formula (
1.7
). We draw the directed lattice in (
1.1
) in a planar form and identify every crossing of the lattice lines with a matrix
\check{R}_{a,b}(z_{a}/(qz_{b}))\in\text{End}(V^{\otimes 2N})
, defined in Section
2.6
4
4

4

We find it convenient to work with
\check{R}=RP
, with
P
being the permutation matrix in
V\otimes V
.
. Then the partition function
Z_{\alpha,\beta}
is given by
5
5

5

In order to recover (
1.1
) one needs to rotate the picture (
1.9
) by
90
degrees counter-clockwise and lay it down on the cone such that the point marked with the dot in (
1.9
) matches with the tip of the cone.

:

(1.9)

where the lines joining the bottom edges with the top edges of the right half of the
N
by
N
lattice are interpreted as the twisted trace in the vector spaces positioned at
N+1\ldots 2N
within the tensor product
V^{\otimes 2N}
. The twist is given by the matrices
\hat{u}
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}u_{i}E_{ii}

(1.10)

which are implied in (
1.9
). The graphical representation of
Z_{N}
is the same as (
1.9
) but with the indices
\alpha
and
\beta
removed.
Algebraically
Z_{N}
reads:

\displaystyle Z_{N}={\text{Tr}}_{N+1\ldots 2N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\check{R}_{N+j-i}\left(\frac{z_{N-i+1}}{qz_{j}}\right)\cdot\text{id}^{\otimes N}\otimes\hat{u}^{\otimes N}\right]

(1.11)

Consider any
X\in\text{End}(V^{\otimes N})(z_{1}\ldots z_{N})
. In Section
3
we show that the assignment:

\displaystyle\Psi\left[X\right]:={\text{Tr}}_{N+1\ldots 2N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\check{R}_{N+j-i}\left(\frac{z_{N-i+1}}{qz_{j}}\right)\cdot\text{id}^{\otimes N}\otimes X\right]

(1.12)

realizes an anti-isomorphism of the matrix shuffle algebras
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}^{+}
given by
X\mapsto\Psi[X]
. Here
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a matrix shuffle algebra which is related to the dual of
\mathcal{A}^{+}
by a simple transformation (see Section
3.1
). The diagonal matrix
\hat{u}^{\otimes N}
in (
1.11
) is a generator of tensor products of diagonal matrix units
E_{ii}
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\otimes N}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{N}}\bigotimes_{i\in\lambda}u_{i}E_{ii}

(1.13)

(see Lemma
3
). Once we show that the elements
X_{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle X_{\lambda}=\text{coefficient of }u_{\lambda_{1}}\cdots u_{\lambda_{N}}\text{ in }\hat{u}^{\otimes N}

(1.14)

belong to the commuting subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, then by the anti-isomorphism property of
\Psi
we will have that
\Psi[X_{\lambda}]\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
for any
\lambda\in\mathcal{I}^{N}
. In the case of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
of quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
it is easy to show that such
X_{\lambda}
are indeed commuting. In Section
4
we explain that this should also hold in the case of quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
, but the details are still conjectural. Therefore the generating function
Z(v)
in (
1.7
) represents a particular example of the application of the anti-isomorphism
\Psi
to the elements (
1.14
) of the commuting subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

1.3.
Formulas for elements of commuting matrix shuffle algebra of the quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}

The generating function (
1.7
) gives rise to many formulas for various families of elements of
\mathcal{B}
. All of these formulas have the form of a partial twisted trace of products of
\check{R}
-matrices similar to (
1.11
). The example discussed above gives such trace formulas for a family of commuting elements labelled by
(\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{N})
with
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda_{i+1}
, namely

\Psi\left[\sum_{\sigma}\bigotimes_{i\in\sigma(\lambda)}E_{ii}\right]

where the sum is over permutations
\sigma
of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
such that
\sigma(\lambda)\neq\lambda
unless
\sigma
is the identity permutation. In this example the tensors
\Psi[X]
,
X
and the product of
\check{R}
’s are all endomorphisms of a tensor product of
N
copies of the same vector space
V
. This is a special case of a more general map:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}[X]={\text{Tr}}_{N+1\ldots 2N}\left[\text{projection}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{N+j-i}\left(\frac{z_{N-i+1}}{qz_{j}}\right)\right)\cdot{\text{id}}^{\otimes N}\otimes X\right]\in\text{End}(V^{\otimes N})

(1.15)

where
X\in\text{End}(V^{\prime\otimes N})
with
V^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n^{\prime}+m^{\prime}}
for some
n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\geqslant 0
. The
R
-matrix
\check{R}^{\prime\prime}
is associated with
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n^{\prime\prime}|m^{\prime\prime}})
with
n^{\prime\prime}\geqslant n
and
m^{\prime\prime}\geqslant m
and acts on
V^{\prime\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}
, with
V^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n^{\prime\prime}+m^{\prime\prime}}
and we identify
V
and
V^{\prime}
as coordinate subspaces of
V^{\prime\prime}
. The projection in (
1.15
) is such that:

\displaystyle\text{projection}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\check{R}^{\prime\prime}_{N+j-i}\left(\frac{z_{N-i+1}}{qz_{j}}\right)\right):\text{End}(V^{\otimes N})\otimes\text{End}(V^{\prime\otimes N})\to\text{End}(V^{\otimes N})\otimes\text{End}(V^{\prime\otimes N})

(1.16)

The trace in (
1.15
) is taken over the
N
tensor factors of the vector spaces
V^{\prime}
. This set up allows one to compute a variety of elements of the commuting subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{+}
that are exponentially generated by
S_{k}^{(i)}
. In Section
4.7
we discuss such examples and in addition we demonstrate how to use
\widetilde{\Psi}
to compute the elements
S_{N}^{(i)}
. The result of this computation is the following formula for the matrix elements
S_{\alpha,\beta}^{(i)}
of
S_{N}^{(i)}
:

\displaystyle S_{\alpha,\beta}^{(i)}=\sum_{\gamma\in\{i+1,i+2\}^{N}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{i+2}(\gamma)}m_{i+2}(\gamma)}{t^{N/2}-t^{-N/2}}\hbox to123.57pt{\vbox to154.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.48857pt\lower-55.84514pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(1.17)

where the crosses are interpreted as the
\check{R}
-matrix of
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n+1|m+1})
, the space
V^{\prime\prime}
has the labeling set
\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}=\{1\ldots i,i+1,i+2,i+3\ldots n+m+2\}
. The gradation on
V^{\prime\prime}
is given by
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{j}=\epsilon_{j}
for
j\leqslant i
,
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}=1
,
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{i+2}=-1
and
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{j}=\epsilon_{j-2}
for
j>i+2
. The map
\iota:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
is given by
\iota(j)=j
for
j\leqslant i
and
\iota(j)=j+2
for
j>i
. Finally
m_{i+2}(\gamma)
is the multiplicity of
i+2
in
\gamma
. Note that the summation over
\gamma
in (
1.17
) can be interpreted as the trace over
N
two dimensional subspaces of
V^{\prime\prime}
.

1.4.
Overview of the paper

In Section
2
, we review the matrix shuffle algebra of type
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
and present its conjectural generalization to type
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
. In Section
3
, we describe the anti-isomorphism
\Psi
(and its relatives such as
\widetilde{\Psi}
) together with its graphical interpretation, which serves as the algebraic bedrock of our main results. In Section
4
, we describe the commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{+}
of the matrix shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
; inside
\mathcal{B}^{+}
we identify the elements
S_{k}^{(i)}
, which allows us to prove Theorem
1
.

2.
The matrix shuffle algebra of quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}

In this section we recall the matrix shuffle algebra and its relation with the quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
algebra
[
19
]
. We will then explain the generalization to the case of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
, of which many steps are conjectural (however, we outline the main challenges that need to be solved). After that we will introduce a diagrammatic representation of the elements of the shuffle algebra and the shuffle product which will be used in subsequent sections.

2.1.
The
R
-matrix

Let
V=\mathbb{C}^{n}
and let
E_{ij}
denote the
n\times n
matrix with a single 1 at the intersection of row
i
and column
j
, and zeroes everywhere else.
The
R
-matrix
R(z/w)\in\text{End}(V_{w}\otimes V_{z})
of
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
is given by:

\displaystyle R(z):=\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{t^{-1/2}-zt^{1/2}}{1-z}\right)^{\delta_{i=j}}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}+\sum_{1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n}\frac{(t^{-1/2}-t^{1/2})z^{\delta_{i<j}}}{1-z}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}

(2.1)

Let
P\in\text{End}(V\otimes V)
be the permutation matrix. The matrix
\check{R}
is defined by:

\displaystyle\check{R}(z):=R(z)P

(2.2)

We define the matrix
\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\in\text{End}(V_{z_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{z_{N}})
by the action of
\check{R}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})
on
V_{z_{i}}\otimes V_{z_{i+1}}
and by the identity on all other tensor factors. Similarly
P_{i}\in\text{End}(V_{z_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{z_{N}})
denotes the action by
P
on
V_{z_{i}}\otimes V_{z_{i+1}}
and by the identity on the other factors. For
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N
we define
\check{R}_{i,j}(z)
as
\check{R}
acting in the
i
-th and
j
-th tensor factors. Another important matrix is
\check{R}^{\bullet}
defined by:

\displaystyle\check{R}^{\bullet}(z):=(D\otimes{\text{id}})\check{R}(zt^{-n})({\text{id}}\otimes D^{-1})

(2.3)

where:

\displaystyle D:={\text{diag}}\{1,t^{-1}\ldots t^{-n+1}\}

(2.4)

The matrix
\check{R}^{\bullet}_{i,j}\in\text{End}(V_{z_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{z_{N}})
is defined in the same way as
\check{R}_{i,j}
.

The matrix
\check{R}
satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation, the unitarity relation and has a simple pole at
z=1
:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{i}(z/y)\check{R}_{i+1}(z/x)\check{R}_{i}(y/x)

\displaystyle=\check{R}_{i+1}(y/x)\check{R}_{i}(z/x)\check{R}_{i+1}(z/y)

(2.5)

\displaystyle\check{R}(x/y)\check{R}(y/x)

\displaystyle=f(x/y)\text{id}\otimes\text{id},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\underset{x=y}{\text{Res}}\,\check{R}(x/y)

\displaystyle=(t^{1/2}-t^{-1/2}){\text{id}}\otimes{\text{id}}

(2.7)

where “Res
f(z)
” denotes the residue of the 1-form
f(z)\frac{dz}{z}
, and

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{(1-tx)(1-t^{-1}x)}{(1-x)^{2}}

(2.8)

Additionally, the matrices
\check{R}
and
\check{R}^{\bullet}
satisfy the
crossing unitarity relation
:

\displaystyle\left(\check{R}^{\bullet}(x)P\right)^{T_{2}}\left(P\check{R}(x^{-1})\right)^{T_{2}}={\text{id}}\otimes{\text{id}}

(2.9)

where
T_{2}
denotes the transposition in the second tensor factor.

2.2.
Symmetric tensors and wheel conditions

Consider the vector space:

\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\otimes k})(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})

(2.10)

For a fixed
k\geqslant 0
we will sometimes write
(z)
instead of
(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
, for simplicity. Similarly, if
\sigma
is a permutation in the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
then we will write
(z_{\sigma})=(z_{\sigma(1)}\ldots z_{\sigma(k)})
. Fix
k\geqslant 0
, a tensor
X=X_{1\dots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
will be called symmetric if for all
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
we have:

\check{R}_{\sigma}(z)X_{1\ldots k}(z)=X_{1\ldots k}(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}(z)

(2.11)

where the matrix
\check{R}_{\sigma}(z)=\check{R}_{\sigma}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
is defined recursively by

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\text{id}}(z)=1,\qquad\check{R}_{\sigma}(z)=\check{R}_{i}(z_{\sigma^{\prime}(i+1)}/z_{\sigma^{\prime}(i)})\check{R}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(z)

(2.12)

whenever
\sigma=s_{i}\sigma^{\prime}
is a reduced decomposition in
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
. We similarly define
\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\sigma}
and
R_{\sigma}
; in particular, in terms of the latter matrices, formula (
2.11
) takes the form

R_{\sigma}(z)X_{1...k}(z)=X_{\sigma(1)\dots\sigma(k)}(z_{\sigma})R_{\sigma}(z)

Above and henceforth, we often write
X_{1\dots k}
to indicate the fact that
X
is a linear operator on
V^{\otimes k}
, and use the notation
X_{\sigma(1)\dots\sigma(k)}
to denote the same linear operator conjugated by the permutation of the factors of
V^{\otimes k}
corresponding to
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
. The subspace of symmetric tensors will be denoted by:

\mathcal{V}_{\text{sym}}\subset\mathcal{V}

(2.13)

Furthermore, we say that a symmetric tensor satisfies the pole conditions if it is of the form:

X_{1\dots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})=\frac{x_{1\dots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})}{\prod_{1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n}(z_{i}-z_{j}q)}

(2.14)

for some Laurent polynomial:

x_{1\dots k}(z_{1}\dots z_{k})\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\otimes k})[z_{1}^{\pm 1}\dots z_{k}^{\pm 1}]

The subspace of symmetric tensors which satisfy the pole conditions will be denoted by:

\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\text{big}}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\text{sym}}

(2.15)

Definition 1
.

Consider any composition
\lambda
of length
u
and size
\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{u}=k
, and let
c_{i}=\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{i-1}+1
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,u+1\}
. A tensor
X\in\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\emph{big}}\cap{\text{End}}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\otimes k})(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
is said to satisfy the wheel conditions if, for every composition
\lambda
as above, the iterated residue of
X
at:

\Big\{z_{\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{s-1}+1}=y_{s},z_{\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{s-1}+2}=y_{s}q\ldots z_{\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{s-1}+\lambda_{s}}=y_{s}q^{\lambda_{s}-1}\Big\}_{1\leqslant s\leqslant u}

(2.16)

is of the form:

(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{k-u}\prod^{\text{unordered pairs }(s,d)\neq(t,e)}_{\text{with }1\leqslant s,t\leqslant u,1\leqslant d<\lambda_{s},1\leqslant e<\lambda_{t}}f\left(\frac{y_{s}q^{d}}{y_{t}q^{e}}\right)\left[\prod_{u\geqslant s\geqslant 1}\prod_{s\leqslant t\leqslant u}\prod_{1\leqslant e<\lambda_{t}}R_{c_{s},c_{t}+e}\left(\frac{y_{s}}{y_{t}q^{e}}\right)\right]

(2.17)

X^{(\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{u})}_{c_{1}\ldots c_{u}}(y_{1}\ldots y_{u})\left[\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant u}\prod_{u\geqslant t>s}\prod_{\lambda_{t}>e\geqslant 1}R_{c_{t}+e,c_{s}}\left(\frac{y_{t}q^{e}}{y_{s}q^{\lambda_{s}}}\right)\right]\left[\prod_{s=1}^{u}P_{c_{s}}P_{c_{s}+1}\dots P_{c_{s+1}-2}\right]

for some tensor
X^{(\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{u})}\in\emph{End}(V^{\otimes u})(y_{1}\ldots y_{u})
. We write:

\mathcal{A}^{+}\subset\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\emph{big}}

(2.18)

for the vector subspace of symmetric tensors which satisfy the pole and wheel conditions.

We remark that the pole conditions (which require that tensors
X
have at most simple poles at
z_{i}=z_{j}q
) were precisely introduced in order for the residue at (
2.16
) to be well-behaved. As shown in
[
19
, Proposition 4.11]
, for any composition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{u})
with
k=\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{u}
, the tensor
X^{\lambda}(y_{1}\ldots y_{u})
that features in (
2.17
) also satisfies the pole conditions in the variables
y_{s}
, and is symmetric in the sense of (
2.11
) with respect to all pairs of variables
y_{s}
and
y_{t}
for which
\lambda_{s}=\lambda_{t}
.

Example 2
.

We consider the particular example of Definition
1
with
u=1
and
k=3
.
and compute the wheel condition for a tensor
X(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{\text{big}}^{+}\cap{\text{End}}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\otimes 3})(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
.
We have
\lambda=(\lambda_{1})=(3)
and
(c_{1},c_{2})=(1,4)
, so the specialization in (
2.16
) is given by:

\left\{z_{1}=y,z_{2}=qy,z_{3}=q^{2}y\right\}

(2.19)

where we set
y_{1}=y
. The first line in (
2.17
) is given by

(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}\prod_{1\leqslant e<d<3}f\left(q^{d-e}\right)\left[\prod_{1\leqslant e<3}R_{1,1+e}\left(q^{-e}\right)\right]=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}f\left(q\right)R_{1,2}\left(q^{-1}\right)R_{1,3}\left(q^{-2}\right)

and the second line in (
2.17
) is given by

X^{(3)}_{1}(y)\left[\prod_{s=1}^{u}P_{c_{s}}P_{c_{s}+1}\dots P_{c_{s+1}-2}\right]=X^{(3)}_{1}(y)P_{1}P_{2}

Combining these two expressions, we conclude that the wheel condition with
\lambda=(3)
for a tensor
X(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
requires that its residue be of the form:

\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y,z_{2}=qy,z_{3}=q^{2}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}X(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}f\left(q\right)R_{1,2}\left(q^{-1}\right)R_{1,3}\left(q^{-2}\right)X^{(3)}_{1}(y)P_{1}P_{2}

(2.20)

for some
X^{(3)}(y)\in\emph{End}(V)(y)
. By commuting the permutation matrices
P_{i}
to the left and combining them with the
R
-matrices we can rewrite this using the
\check{R}
matrices (
2.2
):

\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y,z_{2}=qy,z_{3}=q^{2}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}X(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}f\left(q\right)\check{R}_{1}\left(q^{-1}\right)\check{R}_{2}\left(q^{-2}\right)X^{(3)}_{3}(y)

(2.21)

In a more general case
\lambda=(k)
we have:

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y\ldots z_{k}=q^{k-1}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}X(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{k-1}\prod_{j=1}^{k-2}f\left(q^{j}\right)^{k-1-j}\check{R}_{1}\left(q^{-1}\right)\cdots\check{R}_{k-1}\left(q^{-k+1}\right)X_{k}^{(k)}(y)

(2.22)

2.3.
The shuffle product

We can make the vector space
\mathcal{V}
of (
2.10
) into an associative algebra via the following formula for all
A=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
,
B=B_{1\ldots l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})
in
\mathcal{V}
:

A*B=\sum^{a_{1}<\ldots<a_{k},\ b_{1}<\ldots<b_{l}}_{\{1\ldots k+l\}=\{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}\}\sqcup\{b_{1}\ldots b_{l}\}}\left[\prod_{i=k}^{1}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\underbrace{R_{a_{i}b_{j}}\left(\frac{z_{a_{i}}}{z_{b_{j}}}\right)}_{\text{only if }a_{i}<b_{j}}\right]

(2.23)

A_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}(z_{a_{1}}\ldots z_{a_{k}})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=l}^{1}R_{b_{j}a_{i}}\left(\frac{z_{b_{j}}}{z_{a_{i}}q}\right)\right]B_{b_{1}\ldots b_{l}}(z_{b_{1}}\ldots z_{b_{l}})\left[\prod_{i=k}^{1}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\underbrace{R_{a_{i}b_{j}}\left(\frac{z_{a_{i}}}{z_{b_{j}}}\right)}_{\text{only if }a_{i}>b_{j}}\right]

The unit of this multiplication is
1\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\otimes 0})
. We will refer to
*
as the shuffle product.

Proposition 1

(​​
[
19
, Propositions 2.6, 4.6, 4.10]
)
.

The subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{\emph{sym}}
,
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\emph{big}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{+}
are preserved by
*
.

With the Proposition above in mind, we will call
\mathcal{A}^{+}
the shuffle algebra, and it will be the main object of our study.

The shuffle product
*
can be written in terms of the matrix
\check{R}
:

\displaystyle A*B=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\frac{1}{f_{\sigma}(z)}\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}(z)\Gamma^{+}_{A,B}(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}(z)

(2.24)

where
f_{\sigma}(z)
is a normalizing factor:

\displaystyle f_{\sigma}(z):=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k+l\\
\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)\end{subarray}}f\left(\frac{z_{\sigma(j)}}{z_{\sigma(i)}}\right)

(2.25)

The tensor
\Gamma^{+}_{A,B}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l})
is given by:

\Gamma^{+}_{A,B}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l}):=A_{l+1\ldots k+l}(z_{l+1}\ldots z_{k+l})\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(qz_{l+1}\ldots qz_{l+k},z_{1}\ldots z_{l})\\
B_{k+1\ldots k+l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l})

(2.26)

where
\omega^{j}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}
is the rotation by
j
units:
\omega^{j}(12\ldots k+l)=(k+l-j+1\ldots k+l,1\ldots k+l-j)
.
The version of the shuffle product in (
2.24
) contains pairs of
\check{R}
-matrices which can be removed with the help of the unitarity relation (
2.6
). When one such pair is removed we need to multiply the expression by a factor
f(z_{i}/z_{j})
for an appropriate pair of indices
i,j
. When all such pairs of
\check{R}
-matrices are removed the resulting product of factors
f(z_{i}/z_{j})
will cancel against
f_{\sigma}(z)
.

2.4.
The double shuffle algebra

One of the deeper results concerning the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
(which is one of the reasons why the wheel conditions in Definition
1
are natural to impose) is the following.

Proposition 2
.

(​​
[
19
, Corollary 5.30]
) As a
\mathbb{C}(q,t)
algebra,
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is generated by the elements

E_{ij}=E_{\bar{i}\bar{j}}z_{1}^{\left\lfloor\frac{i-1}{n}\right\rfloor-\left\lfloor\frac{j-1}{n}\right\rfloor}\in\emph{End}(V)[z_{1}^{\pm 1}]

(2.27)

as
(i,j)
runs over
\mathbb{Z}^{2}/(n,n)\mathbb{Z}
, where
\bar{i}
denotes the element of
\{1\ldots n\}
congruent to
i
modulo
n
.

Definition 2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{-}
denote the algebra defined just like
\mathcal{A}^{+}
, but with the parameter
q^{-1}t^{n}
instead of
q
in Definition
1
and (
2.14
), and with

D_{b_{j}}R_{b_{j}a_{i}}\left(\frac{z_{b_{j}}q}{z_{a_{i}}t^{n}}\right)D_{b_{j}}^{-1}\quad\text{instead of}\quad R_{b_{j}a_{i}}\left(\frac{z_{b_{j}}}{z_{a_{i}}q}\right)

in (
2.23
), where
D
was defined in (
2.4
).

We will reuse the symbol
*
for the shuffle product of
\mathcal{A}^{-}
. For two tensors
A=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{A}^{-}
and
B=B_{1\ldots l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})\in\mathcal{A}^{-}
we have the following analogue of (
2.24
)

\displaystyle A*B=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\frac{1}{f_{\sigma}(z)}\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Gamma^{-}_{A,B}(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}

(2.28)

where:

\Gamma^{-}_{A,B}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l}):=A_{l+1\ldots k+l}(z_{l+1}\ldots z_{k+l})\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{l}}(z_{l+1}\ldots z_{l+k},qz_{1}\ldots qz_{l})\\
B_{k+1\ldots k+l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l})

(2.29)

It is easy to see that we have an isomorphism
\mathcal{A}^{+}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}^{-}
given by the assignment

X_{1\dots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\mapsto D_{1}\dots D_{k}X_{1\dots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\Big|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}t^{n}}

In what follows, we will encounter the quantum group of type
\widehat{A}_{n-1}

U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})=\mathbb{C}(t)\Big\langle x_{i}^{+},x_{i}^{-},\psi_{i}^{\pm 1},c^{\pm 1}\Big\rangle_{i\in\{1\ldots n\}}\Big/\Big(\text{\text{relations (3.4)-(3.7) of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Ng_tale}{}{}]}}}\Big)

(2.30)

(note that its Cartan subalgebra has one more dimension than usual for the quantum group, but this is a trivial modification) and the quantum Heisenberg algebra

U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})=\mathbb{C}(t)\Big\langle p_{\pm k},c^{\pm 1}\Big\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Big/\Big([p_{k},p_{-k}]=k\frac{c^{k}-c^{-k}}{t^{k}-t^{-k}}\text{ and all other commutators 0}\Big)

(2.31)

We define the quantum affine group associated to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
by

U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})=U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})\otimes U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})

(2.32)

Definition 3
.

(see
[
19
, Section 6]
for details) The vector space

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{+}\otimes U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{-}

(2.33)

is made into an algebra by specifying how the three factors commute with each other.

2.5.
The quantum toroidal algebra

We will consider the quantum toroidal algebra

U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})=\mathbb{C}(q,t)\Big\langle x_{i,k}^{+},x_{i,k}^{-},\varphi_{i,k^{\prime}}^{+},\varphi_{i,k^{\prime}}^{-},\psi_{i}^{\pm 1},c^{\pm 1},\bar{c}^{\pm 1}\Big\rangle_{i\in\{1\ldots n\}}^{k\in\mathbb{Z},k^{\prime}>0}\Big/\Big(\text{relations (3.41)--(3.47) of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Ng_tale}{}{}]}}\Big)

Theorem 3
.

(​​
[
19
, Theorem 1.5]
) There exists an isomorphism

\Phi:U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}

(2.34)

completely determined by sending

\varphi_{i,1}^{\pm}\in U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})\qquad\text{to}\qquad E_{ii}\in\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\subset\mathcal{A}

and

x_{i,0}^{\pm},\psi_{i}\in U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})\quad\text{to}\quad x_{i}^{\pm},\psi_{i}\in U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})\subset\mathcal{A}

for all
i\in\{1\ldots n\}
.

The isomorphism
\Phi
is very non-trivial (it uses an explicit description of both
U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
and
\mathcal{A}
in terms of slope subalgebras determined in
[
20
]
) and this has a lot of potential, in the following sense: formulas that are quite easy on one side of the isomorphism are quite difficult on the other side, and vice versa. An example of this is given by Proposition
7
in Section
4
. Theorem
3
was proved in
[
16
,
20
,
19
]
by an in-depth study of the algebras involved, in which an important part was played by the classic double shuffle algebra realization of
U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
, which we now recall.

Definition 4
.

(​​
[
4
]
, inspired by
[
9
]
) Consider the vector space

\mathcal{S}^{+}\subset\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{i}\geqslant 0)_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}}\mathbb{C}(q,t)(x_{i1},\dots,x_{id_{i}})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}^{\emph{sym}}

(2.35)

consisting of rational functions which are

•

symmetric in
x_{i1},x_{i2},\dots
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
separately

•

have at most poles of the form
x_{ia}t^{-1/2}-x_{i+1,b}t^{1/2}
for any
i,a,b
, where we write
x_{n+1,b}=q^{-1}x_{1b}
.

•

their residue the pole above is divisible by
x_{ia}-x_{i+1,b^{\prime}}
and by
x_{i+1,b}-x_{ia^{\prime}}
for any
a\neq a^{\prime}
and
b\neq b^{\prime}

One makes
\mathcal{S}^{+}
into an algebra with respect to the shuffle multiplication

R*R^{\prime}=\emph{Sym}\left[R(x_{i1},\dots,x_{id_{i}})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}R^{\prime}(x_{i,d_{i}+1},\dots,x_{i,d_{i}+d_{i}^{\prime}})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\right.\\
\left.\prod_{i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\prod_{a\leqslant d_{i},b>d_{j}}\begin{cases}\frac{x_{ia}t^{-1/2}-x_{jb}t^{1/2}}{x_{ia}-x_{jb}}&\text{if }i\equiv j\text{ mod }n\\
\frac{x_{ia}-x_{jb}q^{-\delta_{j1}}}{x_{ia}t^{-1/2}-x_{jb}t^{1/2}q^{-\delta_{j1}}}&\text{if }i+1\equiv j\text{ mod }n\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\right]

(2.36)

One lets
\mathcal{S}^{-}=\mathcal{S}^{+,\text{op}}
and defines the double shuffle algebra as

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{+}\otimes U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{-}

by appropriately specifying how the three tensor factors should commute with each other (see
[
16
]
for details). Then the main result of
[
16
]
is an isomorphism

\Upsilon:U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}

(2.37)

determined by sending
x_{i,k}^{\pm}
to the Laurent polynomials
x_{i1}^{k}\in\mathcal{S}^{\pm}
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Composing the isomorphisms (
2.34
) and (
2.37
) gives us the isomorphism
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{S}
between the two kinds of double shuffle algebras featured in the present paper.

2.6.
The super case

There exists an analogue of the discussion above for the Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
instead of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
, although much of it is conjectural (we will outline the complete picture and clearly state the missing parts, but stop short of implementing them). One considers
V=\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
and replaces the
R
-matrix (
2.1
) by

\displaystyle R(z):=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+m}\left(\epsilon_{i}\frac{t^{-\epsilon_{i}/2}-zt^{\epsilon_{i}/2}}{1-z}\right)^{\delta_{i=j}}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n+m}\frac{(t^{-1/2}-t^{1/2})z^{\delta_{i<j}}}{1-z}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}

(2.38)

where:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}=(-1)^{\delta_{i>n}}

(2.39)

is the gradation of the standard basis vectors
\left|i\right\rangle\in V
,
i=1\ldots n+m
6
6

6

We choose the gradation of vectors to be
\{\overset{n}{\overbrace{1\ldots 1}},\overset{m}{\overbrace{-1\ldots-1}}\}
for simplicity of presentation. More generally, one may take instead any permutation of
\{1\ldots 1,-1\ldots-1\}
.
.
The supersymmetric version of the matrix
\check{R}
is given by:

\displaystyle\check{R}(z):=R(z)P

(2.40)

The
R
-matrix (
2.38
) has the same fundamental properties as (
2.1
): it satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation with spectral parameter (
2.5
), unitarity (
2.6
) and crossing unitarity (
2.9
) in which the matrix
\check{R}^{\bullet}
reads:

\displaystyle\check{R}^{\bullet}(z):=(D\otimes{\text{id}})\check{R}(zt^{m-n})({\text{id}}\otimes D^{-1})

(2.41)

where:

\displaystyle D:={\text{diag}}\{1,t^{-1}\ldots t^{-n+1},(-1)t^{-n+1},(-t)t^{-n+1}\ldots(-t^{m-1})t^{-n+1}\}

(2.42)

The
R
-matrix (
2.38
) has a simple pole at
z=1
with the same residue as before. This allows the discussion in Subsections
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.4
to run through, and determines shuffle algebras
\mathcal{A}^{+}
and
\mathcal{A}^{-}
that one can combine into a Drinfeld double

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{+}\otimes U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{-}

(2.43)

Conjecture 1
.

There is an isomorphism

\mathcal{A}\cong U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m})

(2.44)

with the quantum toroidal superalgebra in the right-hand side defined as in
[
2
]
.

Remark 1
.

It may be the case that for (
2.44
) to hold, one needs to impose upon its right-hand side additional higher order relations, on top of the ones considered in
[
2
]
. In
[
18
]
(in the particular case of the generalized conifold, which is a singular toric Calabi-Yau threefold) the second-named author introduced such higher order relations, with the express goal of making the quantum toroidal superalgebra isomorphic to the corresponding double shuffle algebra
\mathcal{S}
.

We propose the following approach for the proof of Conjecture
1
, though we point out that working out the details would be a very challenging task (as would be to develop analogues of (
2.44
) in types other than
A
, super or not).

•

Develop the factorization of
U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m})
into slope subalgebras (which we only know how to do using the
\mathcal{S}
interpretation of quantum toroidal algebras, see
[
16
]
for the non-super case).

•

Work out the commutation relations inside
U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n|m})
of elements from “nearby” slope subalgebras (by analogy with the case of quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
that was worked out in
[
3
,
21
]
and of quantum toroidal
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
that was worked out in
[
20
]
).

•

Develop the factorization of
\mathcal{A}
into slope subalgebras, following
[
19
]
.

•

Work out the commutation relations inside
\mathcal{A}
of elements from “nearby” slope subalgebras.

•

Compare the explicit descriptions in bullets 2 and 4, which may require some combinatorial reindexing of generators and non-trivial checking that relations match up.

In the remainder of Section
2
and in
3
, the objects
R(z)
,
\mathcal{A}^{+}
etc will refer to the notions defined with respect to the super Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
, and our main Theorem
1
holds conditional on Conjecture
1
. This Conjecture was proved in
[
19
]
in the non-super case
m=0
, in which case all results contained in the present paper are completely established.

2.7.

V_{z}
is given by a line with the parameter
z
attached to it. The matrix
\check{R}
is represented by a crossing of two lines:

(2.46)

The arrows attached to the vector spaces help us to keep track of the ordering of operators: moving forward w.r.t. the orientation of a line is reading an product of operators right to left. The matrix
\check{R}^{\bullet}
is represented as follows:

(2.50)

A tensor
A\in\text{End}(V^{\otimes k})
will be represented by a box with
k
lines attached to it at the top and
k
lines attached to it at the bottom. These lines represent the
k
tensor factors of
V^{\otimes k}
, and they carry spectral parameters
z_{i}
. In some cases these parameters acquire shifts as they “pass” through
A
. These shifts are necessary features appearing in the matrix shuffle algebras of
U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
, as explained in
[
19
]
. Hence we will encounter two types of tensors:

(2.51)

(2.52)

We note that
\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz_{1}\ldots qz_{k+l})=\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l})
, so the crosses at the top in the diagram (
2.52
) correspond to the factor
\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}
in (
2.26
). We indicated the spectral parameters at the top and at the bottom of this diagram which specifies the shifts of the parameters as they pass through
A
and
B
.

(2.53)

3.
An anti-isomorphism of shuffle algebras

In this section we introduce an anti-isomorphism
\Psi
which relates the shuffle algebras
\mathcal{A}^{+}
and an algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, which is obtained from
\mathcal{A}^{-}
by reversing the order of the tensor product spaces and inverting
q
and
t
. This anti-isomorphism can be extended to a map
\widetilde{\Psi}
relating shuffle algebras associated to different supersymmetric quantum toroidal algebras. In the next section we will use both
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
as tools for producing new commuting elements of matrix shuffle algebras.

3.1.
The shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

We start by defining a transform
\Omega
which acts on tensors in
\mathcal{V}
by:

\displaystyle\Omega(X_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k}))=X_{k\ldots 1}(z_{k}\ldots z_{1})\Big|_{q,t\rightarrow q^{-1},t^{-1}},\qquad X(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}

(3.1)

It is clear that
\Omega(A*B)=\Omega(A)*\Omega(B)
and that
\Omega
is an involution. For any two tensors
A=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
and
B=B_{1\ldots l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})
in
\mathcal{V}
we define:

\Gamma^{\prime}_{A,B}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l}):=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{k}}(z_{k+1}\ldots z_{k+l},qz_{1}\ldots qz_{k})\\
B_{1\ldots l}(z_{k+1}\ldots z_{k+l})\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l})

(3.2)

Lemma 1
.

Let
A=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}
and
B=B_{1\ldots l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})\in\mathcal{V}
and
\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}
be the longest element.
The transform
\Omega
satisfies:

\displaystyle\Omega(\Gamma^{-}_{A,B})=\Gamma^{\prime}_{\Omega(A),\Omega(B)},\qquad\Omega(\check{R}_{\sigma})=\check{R}_{\rho\sigma\rho}

(3.3)

where
\Gamma^{-}
is defined in (
2.29
).

Proof.

The first identity in (
3.3
) follows by applying
\Omega
to the definition of
\Gamma^{-}
and using the second identity in (
3.3
). For
\sigma
a simple transposition the second identity in (
3.3
) is a property of the
\check{R}
-matrix which can be verified directly. Using this and (
2.12
) one verifies the second identity for a general
\sigma
.
∎

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the algebra of tensors
\{\Omega(X)\}_{X\in\mathcal{A}^{-}}
with the multiplication
*^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle A*^{\prime}B=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}}\frac{1}{f_{\sigma}(z)}\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{A,B}(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}

(3.4)

for any
A=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
and
B=B_{1\ldots l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

The shuffle product
*^{\prime}
in (
3.4
) is related to the shuffle product
*
of the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{-}
by the two properties in (
3.3
).
The graphical representation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
is given by:

(3.5)

3.2.
The maps
\Psi
and
\Psi^{\prime}

In this section we define two maps
\Psi
and
\Psi^{\prime}
which we will later show to be algebra isomorphisms. A key ingredient in these two maps is the tensor
\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}
. For two sets of parameters
z=(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
and
w=(w_{1}\ldots w_{l})
and
\omega^{l}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}
we have:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)=\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k},w_{1}\ldots w_{l})=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\check{R}_{l+j-i}\left(\frac{w_{l-i+1}}{z_{j}}\right)

(3.6)

Graphically we can represent
\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}
by:

(3.7)

Thanks to the Yang–Baxter equation, the tensor
\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots,w)\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})

\displaystyle=\check{R}_{l+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(\ldots z_{i},z_{i+1}\ldots,w)

(3.8)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)(z,\ldots w_{j+1},w_{j}\ldots)\check{R}_{k+j}(w_{j+1}/w_{j})

\displaystyle=\check{R}_{j}(w_{j+1}/w_{j})\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,\ldots w_{j},w_{j+1}\ldots)

(3.9)

for
i=1\ldots k-1
and
j=1\ldots l-1
.

Definition 6
.

For any
X_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}
we define two maps:

\displaystyle\Psi\left[X(z)\right]

\displaystyle:={\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)X_{k+1\ldots 2k}(z)\right]

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}\left[X(z)\right]

\displaystyle:={\text{Tr}}_{1\ldots k}\left[\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{k}}(z,qz)X_{1\ldots k}(z)\right]

(3.11)

The graphical representation of (
3.10
) follows from (
2.51
) and (
3.7
), and it may be drawn in the two ways below due to the cyclic property of the trace:

(3.12)

The graphical representation of
\Psi^{\prime}[X(z)]
is analogous. These two maps have several important properties which are outlined in the following Lemma.

Lemma 2
.

For
X(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}
we have the relations:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Psi\left[X(z_{\sigma})\right]\check{R}_{\sigma}=\Psi\left[\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}X(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}\right]

(3.13)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Psi^{\prime}\left[X(z_{\sigma})\right]\check{R}_{\sigma}=\Psi^{\prime}\left[\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}X(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}\right]

(3.14)

If
X
is a symmetric tensor then
\Psi[X(z)]
and
\Psi^{\prime}[X(z)]
are also symmetric tensors:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\sigma}\Psi[X(z)]

\displaystyle=\Psi[X(z_{\sigma})]\check{R}_{\sigma}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\sigma}\Psi^{\prime}[X(z)]

\displaystyle=\Psi^{\prime}[X(z_{\sigma})]\check{R}_{\sigma}

(3.16)

Proof.

It is enough to prove (
3.13
) for all simple transpositions, i.e.:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}\Psi\left[X(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\right]\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})=\Psi\left[\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}X(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\right]

(3.17)

Consider (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) with
l=k
,
j=i
and
z_{a}\rightarrow qz_{a}
and
w_{a}\rightarrow z_{a}
, for
a=1\ldots k
. These two equations can be combined to produce the following identity:

\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(\ldots qz_{i+1},qz_{i}\ldots,\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\\
=\check{R}_{k+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\check{R}_{k+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}

(3.18)

Plugging the formula above in the left hand side of (
3.17
) (and using the definition of
\Psi
in (
3.10
), the identity (
3.18
) and the cyclicity of the trace) gives:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}\Psi\left[X(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\right]\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\check{R}_{k+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\check{R}_{k+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}X_{k+1\ldots 2k}(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\check{R}_{k+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}X_{k+1\ldots 2k}(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\check{R}_{k+i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Psi\left[\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})^{-1}X(\ldots z_{i+1},z_{i}\ldots)\check{R}_{i}(z_{i+1}/z_{i})\right]

This proves (
3.17
), which implies (
3.13
). If
X(z)
is a symmetric tensor then (
3.15
) holds by (
3.13
) and (
2.11
). The proofs of (
3.14
) and (
3.16
) are analogous.
∎

3.3.
A shuffle algebras anti-isomorphism

Here we show that
\Psi
and
\Psi^{\prime}
are anti-isomorphisms and are inverses of each other.

Proposition 3
.

For any tensors
A(z_{1}\ldots z_{l}),B(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}
we have:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}_{\Psi[A],\Psi[B]}=\Psi[\Gamma^{\prime}_{B,A}]

(3.19)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{\Psi^{\prime}[A],\Psi^{\prime}[B]}=\Psi^{\prime}[\Gamma^{+}_{B,A}]

(3.20)

Additionally we have:

\displaystyle\Psi[\Psi^{\prime}[A(z)]]=\Psi^{\prime}[\Psi[A(z)]]=A(z)

(3.21)

Proof.

We give a graphical proof.
Using (
2.52
) and (
3.12
) we can represent
\Gamma^{+}_{\Psi[A],\Psi[B]}
as:

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~~~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Gamma_prime-graph},\eqref{eq:Psi_graph}}}{{=}}\Psi[\Gamma^{\prime}_{B,A}]

In the second equality we used the Yang–Baxter equation repeatedly. This concludes the proof of (
3.19
). The proof of the second equation (
3.20
) is analogous. The proof of (
3.21
) uses the same ideas as the proof of Lemma
4
, we leave it to the interested reader.
∎

Corollary 1
.

There exists an anti-isomorphism of shuffle algebras
\Psi:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}^{+}
given by:

\displaystyle X\mapsto\Psi[X]

(3.22)

with inverse
\Psi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
A(z_{1}\ldots z_{l}),B(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. We apply
\Psi
to the shuffle product
*^{\prime}
:

\Psi[A*^{\prime}B]=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\Psi[\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{A,B}(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}]=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Psi[\Gamma^{\prime}_{A,B}(z_{\sigma})]\check{R}_{\sigma}\\
=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\check{R}_{\sigma}^{-1}\Gamma^{+}_{\Psi[B],\Psi[A]}(z_{\sigma})\check{R}_{\sigma}=\Psi[B]*\Psi[A]

(3.23)

where we used (
3.4
), (
3.13
), (
3.19
) and (
2.24
), respectively. This implies that
\Psi
and
\Psi^{\prime}
give mutually inverse algebra anti-homomorphisms between
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. The map
\Psi
sends elements of
\text{End}(V)(z_{1}^{\pm 1})
to elements of
\text{End}(V)(z_{1}^{\pm 1})
and therefore by Proposition
2
this implies that
\Psi
sends
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{A}^{+}
.
∎

3.4.
Shuffle algebras’ anti-homomorphisms

In this section we introduce linear maps
\widetilde{\Psi}
which arise from composing
\Psi
with projections. These linear maps become anti-homomorphisms under certain conditions.

Recall the vector space
V\cong\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
and consider two vectors spaces
V^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n^{\prime}+m^{\prime}}
and
V^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n^{\prime\prime}+m^{\prime\prime}}
such that
V
and
V^{\prime}
are two coordinate subspaces of
V^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
denote the label sets of the basis vectors of
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Fix two projections
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
and embeddings
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\pi:V^{\prime\prime}\to V,\qquad\pi^{\prime}:V^{\prime\prime}\to V^{\prime},\qquad\iota:V\to V^{\prime\prime},\qquad\iota^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime\prime}

(3.24)

such that
\pi
removes
n^{\prime\prime}-n
standard basis vectors with bosonic grading and
m^{\prime\prime}-m
standard basis vectors with fermionic grading and similarly
\pi^{\prime}
removes
n^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}
standard basis vectors with bosonic grading and
m^{\prime\prime}-m^{\prime}
standard basis vectors with fermionic grading. The embeddings
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
act as inverses on the corresponding subspaces. The maps
\pi,\pi^{\prime},\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
can be reused to denote the maps of the labeling sets by replacing
V
with
\mathcal{I}
in (
3.24
). In particular, the maps
\pi(\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime})
and
\pi^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime})
are order preserving and we have
\iota(\mathcal{I})\cup\iota^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
R^{(V^{\prime})}
and
R^{(V^{\prime\prime})}
be the
R
-matrices of
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n^{\prime}|m^{\prime}})
and
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n^{\prime\prime}|m^{\prime\prime}})
acting on
V^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Let
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
, with
i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, and
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, with
i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
, be the gradings of the standard basis vectors of
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. We can write:

\displaystyle(\pi\otimes\pi)R^{(V^{\prime\prime})}(z)(\iota\otimes\iota)=R(z),\qquad(\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime})R^{(V^{\prime\prime})}(z)(\iota^{\prime}\otimes\iota^{\prime})=R^{(V^{\prime})}(z)

(3.25)

where we require that
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\iota(i)}=\epsilon_{i}
and
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\iota^{\prime}(i)}=\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
.
The same relations hold for
\check{R}
. Let:

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\prime\otimes k})(z_{1}\ldots z_{k}),\qquad\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}(V^{\prime\prime\otimes k})(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})

(3.26)

Consider two tensors
A=A_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
and
B=B_{1\ldots l}(z_{1}\ldots z_{l})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
which preserve the coordinate subspaces
V^{\otimes k}
and
V^{\otimes l}
, respectively. Using (
3.25
) one can show that:

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes(k+l)}\left(A*^{\prime\prime}B\right)\iota^{\otimes(k+l)}=\left(\pi^{\otimes k}A\iota^{\otimes k}\right)*\left(\pi^{\otimes l}B\iota^{\otimes l}\right)

(3.27)

where
*^{\prime\prime}
denotes the shuffle product (
2.23
) with
R
replaced by
R^{(V^{\prime\prime})}
. Let us stress that the space
V^{\prime\prime}
is used as an auxiliary space and the gradation of the standard basis vectors of
V^{\prime\prime}
does not need to be ordered as
\{1\ldots 1,-1\ldots-1\}
.

Definition 7
.

For
X(z)=X_{1\ldots k}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
we define the map:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[X(z)\right]=\pi^{\otimes k}\left({\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\right)\left({\text{id}}_{V^{\prime\prime}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\left(\iota^{\prime\otimes k}X(z)\pi^{\prime\otimes k}\right)\right)\right]\right)\iota^{\otimes k}

(3.28)

where the traces are taken over
k
copies of
V^{\prime\prime}
.

In (
3.28
) the non-trivial contribution from the trace only comes from the subspaces
V^{\prime}
. An equivalent definition can be given by writing:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[X(z)\right]:={\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\otimes\pi^{\prime\otimes k}\right)\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\right)\left(\iota^{\otimes k}\otimes\iota^{\prime\otimes k}\right)X_{k+1\ldots 2k}(z)\right]\in\mathcal{V}

(3.29)

where the traces are taken over
k
copies of
V^{\prime}
. The graphical formula of (
3.28
) is:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[X(z)\right]=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in\iota(\mathcal{I})^{k}}\left|\pi(\alpha)\right\rangle\left\langle\pi(\beta)\right|\sum_{\gamma,\delta\in\iota^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})^{k}}\hbox to180.52pt{\vbox to142.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.29643pt\lower-50.15466pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
