(3.30)

where each cross is interpreted as
\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}(z_{i}/(qz_{j}))
. In this diagram we removed the trace lines and instead wrote the summations explicitly in order to emphasize that the trace is taken over the non-trivial subspace of
\iota^{\prime}(V^{\prime})
. When we choose projections in (
3.24
), we will always require that
\iota(\mathcal{I})\cup\iota^{\prime}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})=\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
, otherwise the space
V^{\prime\prime}
will contain extra dimensions which will play no role in
\widetilde{\Psi}\left[X(z)\right]
.

Remark 2
.

Consider the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
from Definition
7
applied to
X\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
(i.e. take
V^{\prime}=V^{\prime\prime}
in (
3.24
)). Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the shuffle algebra from Definition
5
(but associated to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n^{\prime\prime}|m^{\prime\prime}}
) and
\mathcal{A}^{+}
be the shuffle algebra from Definition
1
(associated to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
). There exists an anti-homomorphism of shuffle algebras
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}^{+}
given by:

\displaystyle X\mapsto\widetilde{\Psi}[X]

(3.31)

The statement holds because under the conditions of the Remark the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[X(z)\right]=\pi^{\otimes k}\left({\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\right)\left({\text{id}}_{V^{\prime\prime}}^{\otimes k}\otimes X(z)\right)\right]\right)\iota^{\otimes k}

(3.32)

is a composition of the anti-isomorphism
\Psi
(acting in
\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
) and the projection which is a homomorphism due to (
3.27
). However, if we take
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
different than
\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
in Remark
2
then
\widetilde{\Psi}
fails to be an anti-homomorphism. This happens because the inner part of the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
in (
3.28
) is given by
\iota^{\prime\otimes^{k}}\bullet\pi^{\prime\otimes^{k}}
which does not respect the shuffle algebras’ multiplication. As will be explained in the next section there exist exceptions
X,Y\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
for which:

\widetilde{\Psi}[X*^{\prime}Y]=\widetilde{\Psi}[X]*\widetilde{\Psi}[Y]

4.
The commutative subalgebra

This section is devoted to the commutative subalgebra of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
. Our main goal is to derive trace formulas for several families of commuting elements using the anti-isomorphism
\Psi
and the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
. As a consequence we prove Theorem
1
and equation (
1.17
) from the Introduction.

4.1.
Slope 0

The shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
comes endowed with two gradings, which we will refer to as vertical and horizontal. In order to define them, we recall the fact that any element of
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is a linear combination of tensors:

f(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})E_{i_{1}j_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{i_{k}j_{k}}

(4.1)

for various rational functions
f
and indices
i_{1}\ldots i_{k},j_{1}\ldots j_{k}\in\{1\ldots n\}
. We then define the
vertical
and
horizontal
gradings by the following formulas:

\displaystyle\text{vdeg }X=k\in\mathbb{N}

(4.2)

\displaystyle\text{hdeg }X=(\text{hom deg }f)\boldsymbol{\delta}-\sum_{s=1}^{k}[i_{s};j_{s})\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}

(4.3)

where hom deg denotes the homogeneous degree of a rational function,
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(1\ldots 1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}
, and

[i;j)=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{i}+\dots+\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{j-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}

with
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{k}
being the
n
-tuple of integers with a single 1 on position
\equiv k
modulo
n
, and 0 everywhere else. While the definition above makes sense only for
i\leqslant j
, we extend it to all integers
i
and
j
by setting
[i;j)+[j;i)=0
. We will write

\mathcal{A}^{+}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{k},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{k}=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}}\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{d},k}

for the graded pieces of the shuffle algebra, and we henceforth focus on the
\boldsymbol{d}=0
direct summand.

Definition 8
.

An element of
\mathcal{A}_{0,k}
is said to have slope 0 if it is a linear combination of tensors of the form (
4.1
) such that

\deg_{\xi}f(z_{1}\ldots z_{\ell},\xi z_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi z_{k})\cdot n-\sum_{a=\ell+1}^{k}(j_{a}-i_{a})\leqslant 0

(4.4)

for all
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
, where
\deg_{\xi}
refers to the order of a rational function in
\xi
as
\xi\rightarrow\infty
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{k}\subset\mathcal{A}_{0,k}
be the subspace of tensors satisfying the property in Definition
8
. For all
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
, we may define the operation

\text{lead}_{\ell}:\mathcal{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{k-\ell}

(4.5)

by the following procedure: first write any element
\mathcal{B}_{k}
as a linear combination of tensors (
4.1
). Then change the variables of
f
according to
z_{\ell+1}\mapsto\xi z_{\ell+1},\dots,z_{k}\mapsto\xi z_{k}
and retain only the coefficient of

\xi^{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{a=l+1}^{k}(j_{a}-i_{a})}

i.e. the greatest possible power of
\xi
allowed by inequality (
4.4
). The resulting expression in variables
z_{1}\ldots z_{k}
can be interpreted as an element of
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{k-\ell}
, where the variables
z_{1}\ldots z_{\ell}
(respectively
z_{\ell+1}\ldots z_{k}
) correspond to the first (respectively second) tensor factor.

It was shown in
[
19
, Proposition 5.8]
that

\mathcal{B}^{+}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{k}

(4.6)

is preserved by the shuffle product
*
. The following result shows that
\mathcal{B}^{+}
is commutative.

Proposition 4
.

(​​
[
19
, Proposition 5.23]
) There exists an algebra isomorphism

\Xi:\Lambda^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{B}^{+}

(4.7)

where
\Lambda=\mathbb{C}(q,t)[p_{1},p_{2},\dots]
is the ring of symmetric polynomials in infinitely many variables.

4.2.
Explicitly unraveling
\Xi

The isomorphism (
4.7
) was defined in
[
19
]
by placing bialgebra structures on (enlargements of) both
\Lambda^{\otimes n}
and
\mathcal{B}^{+}
and then constructing explicit elements of the latter algebra which correspond under
\Xi
to complete symmetric functions. However, it is more natural to use power sum functions instead. To this end, consider the element

P_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}

(4.8)

that corresponds to the power-sum function
p_{k}
in the
i
-th tensor factor under the isomorphism
\Xi
. Explicitly, the way these elements were constructed in
[
19
]
is by requiring that

P_{k}^{(i)}\text{ has the property that the inequality \eqref{eqn:inequality} is strict},\ \forall\ell\in\{1\ldots k-1\}

(4.9)

The vector space of elements of
\mathcal{B}_{k}
with the property in (
4.9
) is
n
-dimensional, and thus one has the freedom to transform the vector

\left(P_{k}^{(1)}\dots P_{k}^{(n)}\right)\in\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\oplus n}

since it is only determined up to a linear transformation in
GL_{n}
. To completely determine the vector above, we consider the following.

Definition 9
.

For any
i\in\{1\ldots n\}
, consider the linear map

\alpha_{i}:\mathcal{B}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(q,t)

\alpha_{i}(X)=\Big(\text{coefficient of }E_{ii}\text{ in }X^{(k)}(y)\text{ of \eqref{eqn:residue (k)}}\Big)

for all
X\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
(note that
\alpha_{i}(X)=\alpha_{[i;i)}(X)\cdot(1-t^{-2})^{k}q^{\frac{k(2i-1)}{n}}
in the notation of
[
19
]
).

As shown in
[
19
, Corollary 5.13]
, the map
\alpha_{i}
is an algebra homomorphism for all
i\in\{1\ldots n\}
. We completely determine the elements (
4.8
) by property (
4.9
) together with the following normalization

\alpha_{j}\left(P_{k}^{(i)}\right)=\delta_{ij},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}

(4.10)

The following result is key to constructing interesting elements in the commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{+}
.

Proposition 5
.

Suppose we have elements
\{H_{k}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
such that
H_{0}=1
and

\emph{lead}_{\ell}\left(H_{k}\right)=H_{\ell}\otimes H_{k-\ell}

(recall the notation (
4.5
)) for all
\ell\in\{0\ldots k\}
. Then we have the following equality of generating series

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}x^{k}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\gamma^{k}_{(i)}}{k}\cdot P_{k}^{(i)}x^{k}\right)

(4.11)

for some constants
\{\gamma^{k}_{(i)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N},i\in\{1\ldots n\}}\in\mathbb{C}(q,t)
.

Proposition
4
allows us to reduce Proposition
5
to the analogous statement for
\Lambda^{\otimes n}
(with the analogue of
\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\text{lead}_{\ell}
being the Hall coproduct on
\Lambda
) in which case the result is well known. Moreover, because the maps
\alpha_{i}
are algebra homomorphisms, the constants
\gamma^{k}_{(i)}
can be determined from the equalities

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}(H_{k})x^{k}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{k}_{(i)}}{k}x^{k}\right),\quad\forall i\in\{1\ldots n\}

(4.12)

We make the convention that
\alpha_{i}(H_{0})=1
for all
i
.

4.3.
Elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
part I

In the present paper, a key role is played by the following elements:

H_{k}^{(i)}=\underbrace{E_{ii}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{ii}}_{k\text{ factors}}\in\mathcal{V}

(4.13)

Define their generating series:

\displaystyle H^{(i)}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}^{(i)}x^{k}

(4.14)

Proposition 6
.

We have the following equality of generating series:

H^{(i)}(x)=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{P_{k}^{(i)}(-1)^{k-1}}{k}x^{k}\right)

(4.15)

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Proof.

Since it is easy to show that
H_{k}^{(i)}
commutes with
\check{R}_{i,j}
for all
1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant k
, then
H_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{V}_{\text{sym}}
. Since
E_{ii}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{ii}
has no denominators, its residue at
z_{i}=z_{j}q
is equal to 0 for all
k\geqslant 2
for trivial reasons, so we have

H_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}

Clearly,
H_{k}^{(i)}
has vertical degree
k
and horizontal degree 0. Moreover, it has slope 0 because the left-hand side of (
4.4
) for
E_{ii}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{ii}
is equal to 0 for all
\ell\in\{0\ldots k\}
. This implies not only that

H_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}

but that the elements
\{H_{k}^{(i)}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
5
. Therefore, we have a power series equality of the form (
4.11
), and it remains to compute its coefficients. Because

\left(H_{1}^{(i)}\right)^{(1)}=E_{ii},\qquad\left(H_{k}^{(i)}\right)^{(k)}=0,\ \forall k\geqslant 2

(the latter property follows because the residues (
2.17
) are 0 for
E_{ii}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{ii}
) then we have

\alpha_{j}(H_{k}^{(i)})=\delta_{k0}+\delta_{k1}\delta_{ij}

Plugging this into formula (
4.12
) yields

1+x\delta_{ij}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{k}_{(j)}}{k}x^{k}\right),\quad\forall j\in\{1,\dots,n\}

Taking the logarithm implies that
\gamma^{k}_{(j)}=\delta_{ij}(-1)^{k-1}
, which implies formula (
4.15
).

∎

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{N})\in\{1\ldots n\}^{N}
be any sequence and
m(\lambda)=(m_{1}(\lambda)\ldots m_{n}(\lambda))
be the multiplicity vector of
\lambda
, i.e.
m_{i}(\lambda)
denotes the multiplicity of
i
in
\lambda
.

Lemma 3
.

Let
k_{1}\ldots k_{n}\geqslant 0
such that
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=N
. We have the following identity:

\displaystyle H_{k_{1}}^{(1)}*\cdots*H_{k_{n}}^{(n)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\{1\ldots n\}^{N}\\
m(\lambda)=(k_{1}\ldots k_{n})\end{subarray}}E_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{\lambda_{N}\lambda_{N}}

(4.16)

Proof.

We will follow the proof of Proposition
6
in order to prove (
4.16
) by induction on
k_{1}+\dots+k_{n}=N
. To this end, we must first show that the left and right-hand sides of (
4.16
) have the same value of
\text{lead}_{M}
for any
M\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
. Indeed,
\text{lead}_{M}(\text{LHS})
is equal to

\sum_{\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{n}=M}\Big(H_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}*\dots*H_{\ell_{n}}^{(n)}\Big)\otimes\Big(H_{k_{1}-\ell_{1}}^{(1)}*\dots*H_{k_{n}-\ell_{n}}^{(n)}\Big)

(this uses the multiplicativity of
\sum_{\ell}\text{lead}_{M}
, which was established in
[
19
]
), while
\text{lead}_{\ell}(\text{RHS})
is equal to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\{1\ldots n\}^{N}\\
m(\lambda)=(k_{1}\ldots k_{n})\end{subarray}}\Big(E_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{\lambda_{M}\lambda_{M}}\Big)\otimes\Big(E_{\lambda_{M+1}\lambda_{M+1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{\lambda_{N}\lambda_{N}}\Big)

The two displays above are equal for all
M\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
by the induction hypothesis. Finally, we must show that the LHS and the RHS have the same values under the linear maps
\alpha_{i}
of Definition
9
. Since these linear maps are multiplicative, we have

\alpha_{i}(\text{LHS})=\alpha_{i}(H_{k_{1}}^{(1)})\cdots\alpha_{i}(H_{k_{n}}^{(n)})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }(k_{1},\dots,k_{i},\dots,k_{n})=(0,\dots,0,\dots,0)\\
1&\text{if }(k_{1},\dots,k_{i},\dots,k_{n})=(0,\dots,1,\dots,0)\\
0&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}

Meanwhile, because the RHS of (
4.16
) has no poles at
z_{i}-z_{j}q
, then
\alpha_{i}(\text{RHS})
is non-zero only if
N=0
or if
N=1
and
\lambda_{1}=i
. In either case, we see that the left and right-hand sides of equation (
4.16
) have the same value under the linear maps
\alpha_{i}
, thus implying that they are equal.

∎

4.4.
Elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
part II

We will now give a new description of the generators
P_{k}^{(i)}
of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
.

Proposition 7
.

The elements
P_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\subset\mathcal{A}_{k}
are completely determined by the linear equations

\begin{cases}P_{k}^{(1)}=q^{k}S_{k}^{(n)}-S_{k}^{(1)}\\
P_{k}^{(2)}=S_{k}^{(1)}-S_{k}^{(2)}\\
\dots\\
P_{k}^{(n)}=S_{k}^{(n-1)}-S_{k}^{(n)}\end{cases}

(4.17)

where
\{S_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\subset\emph{End}(V^{\otimes k})(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N},i\in\{1\ldots n\}}
are recursively determined by the properties

(z_{1}+\dots+z_{k})S_{k}^{(i)}=\left[S_{k-\ell}^{(i)},(z_{1}+\dots+z_{\ell})S_{\ell}^{(i)}\right]

(4.18)

for all
i\in\{1\ldots n\}
and
k>\ell\geqslant 1
, as well as the initial conditions

\displaystyle S_{1}^{(i)}=\frac{-1}{1-q}\sum_{j=1}^{n}q^{\delta_{j>i}}E_{jj}

(4.19)

(which is simply restating (
4.17
) for
k=1
, given that
P_{1}^{(i)}=E_{ii}
).

Proof.

First of all, we observe that the operation

\delta:\mathcal{A}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{+},\quad\delta(X(z_{1}\ldots z_{k}))=(z_{1}+\dots+z_{k})X(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})

is a derivation with respect to the shuffle product, i.e.

\delta(X*X^{\prime})=\delta(X)*X^{\prime}+X*\delta(X^{\prime})

However, commutators are also derivations, so we claim that for all
X\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
we have

\delta(X)=[X,p_{1}]

(4.20)

where
p_{1}
is an element of
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})\subset U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
, embedded in
\mathcal{A}
by the middle tensor factor in (
2.33
). To prove (
4.20
), one only needs to check this formula on the generators
E_{ij}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
(see Proposition
2
), in which case it is a direct application of
[
19
, formulas (3.77) and (6.36)]
. Note that for the latter statement to be true on the nose, we need to renormalize our generators according to

\Big(p_{1}\text{ of \eqref{eqn:gl 1}}\Big)=\left(\frac{p_{\boldsymbol{\delta},1}^{(0)}}{q^{-1}-q}\text{ of \emph{loc. cit.}}\right)

With formula (
4.20
) in mind, formula (
4.18
) becomes equivalent to the following equality

\left[S_{k}^{(i)},p_{1}\right]=\left[S_{k-\ell}^{(i)},[S_{\ell}^{(i)},p_{1}]\right]

(4.21)

in
\mathcal{A}
. To prove (
4.21
), it is enough to prove it under the isomorphism

\Upsilon\circ\Phi^{-1}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}

with respect to (
2.34
) and (
2.37
). It was shown in
[
16
]
that, under the isomorphism
\Upsilon
,
p_{1}
corresponds to the rational function

f(x_{11}\ldots x_{n1})=\frac{\gamma\cdot x_{11}\dots x_{n1}}{(x_{11}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-x_{21}t^{\frac{1}{2}})\dots(x_{n-1,1}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-x_{n1}t^{\frac{1}{2}})(x_{n1}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-x_{11}t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-1})}

(4.22)

in the
\boldsymbol{\delta}=(1\ldots 1)
direct summand of (
2.35
). The constant
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}(q,t)^{*}
is not important for us, as one can always compose the isomorphism
\Upsilon
by an appropriate renormalization of the generators of
U_{q,t}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
. Moreover, in
\mathcal{S}
the following equalities were proved in
[
20
, formulas (3.23) and (3.105)]

\begin{split}&\left[P_{k}^{(1)},R\right]=\left(q^{k}\Big(x_{n1}^{k}+\dots+q^{k}x_{nd_{n}}^{k}\Big)-\Big(x_{11}^{k}+\dots+x_{1d_{1}}^{k}\Big)\right)R\\
&\left[P_{k}^{(2)},R\right]=\left(\Big(x_{11}^{k}+\dots+x_{1d_{1}}^{k}\Big)-\Big(x_{21}^{k}+\dots+x_{2d_{2}}^{k}\Big)\right)R\\
&\dots\\
&\left[P_{k}^{(n)},R\right]=\left(\Big(x_{n-1,1}^{k}+\dots+x_{n-1,d_{n-1}}^{k}\Big)-\Big(x_{d1}^{k}+\dots+x_{nd_{n}}^{k}\Big)\right)R\end{split}

(4.23)

for all
R=R(x_{i1}\ldots x_{id_{i}})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\in\mathcal{S}^{+}
. The linear combinations (
4.17
) were chosen so that

\left[S_{k}^{(i)},R\right]=\left(x_{i1}^{k}+\dots+x_{id_{i}}^{k}\right)R

(4.24)

for all
i\in\{1\ldots n\}
. Thus, formula (
4.21
) is a consequence of the fact that the function
f=f(x_{11}\ldots x_{n1})
of (
4.22
) (as does any function in the
(1\ldots 1)
direct summand of (
2.35
)) satisfies

x_{i1}^{k}\cdot f=x_{i1}^{k-\ell}\cdot x_{i1}^{\ell}\cdot f

(4.25)

Thus, the trivial equality (
4.25
) in
\mathcal{S}
gives rise to the quite non-trivial equality (
4.21
) in
\mathcal{A}
.

∎

Remark 3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the slope
0
subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
We can define the elements:

H_{k}^{\prime(i)}=\underbrace{E_{ii}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{ii}}_{k\text{ factors}}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

(4.26)

Let
k_{1}\ldots k_{n}\geqslant 0
such that
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=N
.
By a similar logic to that of Lemma
3
, we have

\displaystyle H_{k_{1}}^{\prime(1)}*^{\prime}\cdots*^{\prime}H_{k_{n}}^{\prime(n)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\{1\ldots n\}^{N}\\
m(\lambda)=(k_{1}\ldots k_{n})\end{subarray}}E_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{\lambda_{N}\lambda_{N}}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

(4.27)

4.5.
The super case

As we explained in Subsection
2.6
, the
R
-matrix (
2.38
) is so similar to (
2.1
) that many of the definitions and basic properties of shuffle algebras carry through. In fact, the only place where the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
case exhibits a difference is in the fact that the
R
-matrix (
2.38
) has the property that

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}R(z)=\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n+m}\left(\epsilon_{i}t^{\epsilon_{i}/2}\right)^{\delta_{i=j}}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}+\sum_{1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n+m}t^{1/2}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}

(4.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}R(z)=\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n+m}\left(\epsilon_{i}t^{-\epsilon_{i}/2}\right)^{\delta_{i=j}}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}+\sum_{1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n+m}t^{-1/2}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}

(4.29)

whereas the respective limits for the
R
-matrices (
2.1
) only involved those
i
’s with
\epsilon_{i}=1
. The fact that the “diagonal” terms in the limits above depend on
\epsilon_{i}
is precisely accounted for in the commutation properties of the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
(specifically, formula (5.8) of
[
19
]
would need to be adapted in order to capture the dependence on
\epsilon_{i}
). Therefore, the definition of the slope 0 subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{+}
in Subsection
4.1
runs through without modification, and the following analogue of Proposition
4
holds:

\Xi:\Lambda^{\otimes n+m}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{B}^{+}

(4.30)

Moreover, the natural analogues of Proposition
5
and
6
also hold: the elements

H_{k}^{(i)}=\underbrace{E_{ii}\otimes\dots\otimes E_{ii}}_{k\text{ factors}},\qquad\forall i\in\{1,\dots,n+m\},k>0

lie in
\mathcal{B}^{+}
, and have the property that

H^{(i)}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}^{(i)}x^{k}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{P_{k}^{(i)}(-1)^{k-1}}{k}x^{k}\right)

for certain “power-sum” generators
\{P_{k}^{(i)}\}^{1\leqslant i\leqslant n+m}_{k>0}
. However, we do not have a proof of Proposition
7
in the super case, since it relies on the conjectural isomorphism (
2.44
). Thus, while the features of the commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{+}\cong\Lambda^{\otimes(n+m)}
run completely parallel in the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
as in the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
case, those properties which require us to understand the entire matrix shuffle algebra (such as (
2.44
)) are still conjectural in the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}
case.

4.6.
Computing new elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
using the anti-isomorphism
\Psi

The basic elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
, which were discussed in Section
4.3
, have counterparts in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. One such example, given in Remark
3
, is the family
H^{\prime(j)}_{k}
. In this section we apply the isomorphism
\Psi:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{+}
from Section
3
to the elements
H^{\prime(j)}_{k}
in order to describe a new family of commuting elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
. As a consequence we prove Theorem
1
.

Theorem 4
.

Fix
j\in\{1\ldots n+m\}
. The action of
\Psi
on the generating function
H^{\prime(j)}(x)
, defined by:

\displaystyle\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}(x)\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}_{k}\right]x^{k}

(4.31)

is given by the formula:

\displaystyle\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}(x)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\frac{\epsilon_{j}^{k}}{k}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}k/2}-q^{\delta_{i=j}k}t^{-\epsilon_{j}k/2}}{1-q^{k}}q^{k\delta_{i>j}}P_{k}^{(i)}x^{k}\right)

(4.32)

Proof.

Since
\Psi
is an isomorphism, it satisfies the conditions of Proposition
5
and we must have:

\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}(x)\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]x^{k}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\frac{\gamma^{k}_{(i)}}{k}\cdot P_{k}^{(i)}x^{k}\right)

(4.33)

for some coefficients
\gamma^{k}_{(i)}
. We can compute these coefficients using evaluations as in (
4.12
):

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)x^{k}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{k}_{(i)}}{k}x^{k}\right),\quad\forall i\in\{1\ldots n+m\}

(4.34)

The evaluations
\alpha_{i}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)
are computed in Lemma
5
in Appendix
B
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)=\begin{cases}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}&i=j\\
q^{k\delta_{i>j}}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l-1}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}&i\neq j\end{cases}

For
i=j
(
4.34
) becomes:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)x^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}

(4.35)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\epsilon_{j}^{k}}{k}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}k/2}-q^{k}t^{-\epsilon_{j}k/2}}{1-q^{k}}x^{k}\right)

and for
i\neq j
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)x^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l-1}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}q^{k\delta_{i>j}}

(4.36)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\epsilon_{j}^{k}}{k}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}k/2}-t^{-\epsilon_{j}k/2}}{1-q^{k}}q^{k\delta_{i>j}}x^{k}\right)

where we computed the infinite sums in (
4.35
) and (
4.36
) using the
q
-binomial theorem.
With these two formulas we find all
\gamma_{(i)}^{k}
from (
4.33
) which gives us (
4.32
).
∎

Remark 4
.

We can express the exponential formula (
4.32
) in terms of the generators
S_{k}^{(j)}
:

\displaystyle\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}(x)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{j}^{k}\left(t^{-\epsilon_{j}k/2}S_{k}^{(j-1)}-t^{\epsilon_{j}k/2}S_{k}^{(j)}\right)\right)

(4.37)

where
S_{k}^{(0)}:=q^{k}S_{k}^{(n+m)}
.

Proof.

The expression in (
4.37
) follows from the substitution (
4.17
) into (
4.32
) and a further simplification.
∎

Corollary 2

(Theorem
1
)
.

The generating function
Z(v)
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle Z(v)=\exp\left(\sum_{k>0}\frac{v^{k}}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\left(w_{i+1}^{k}-t^{\epsilon_{i}k}w_{i}^{k}\right)S_{k}^{(i)}\right)

(4.38)

where
w_{i}:=t^{-\epsilon_{i}/2}\epsilon_{i}u_{i}
and
u_{n+m+1}:=qu_{1}
.

Proof.

Let us recall the expression (
1.11
) for
Z_{N}
from the introduction:

\displaystyle Z_{N}(u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m})={\text{Tr}}_{N+1\ldots 2N}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\check{R}_{N+j-i}\left(\frac{z_{N-i+1}}{qz_{j}}\right)\cdot\text{id}^{\otimes N}\otimes\hat{u}^{\otimes N}\right]

(4.39)

where we made explicit the dependence of
Z_{N}
on the parameters
u_{i}
and we recall
\hat{u}
:

\displaystyle\hat{u}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}u_{i}E_{ii}

(4.40)

The expression
\hat{u}^{\otimes N}
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\otimes N}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa=(\kappa_{1}\ldots\kappa_{n+m}):\\
|\kappa|=N\end{subarray}}u_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots u_{n+m}^{\kappa_{n+m}}\sum_{\lambda:m(\lambda)=\kappa}E_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes E_{\lambda_{N}\lambda_{N}}

(4.41)

By Remark
3
, we can view the coefficients of the monomials in
u_{i}
in the above formula as elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
Hence we can apply the anti-isomorphism
\Psi
to each of these coefficients. By comparing the definition of
\Psi
in (
3.10
) with
Z_{N}
in (
4.39
) we conclude that:

\displaystyle Z_{N}(u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m})=\Psi\left[\hat{u}^{\otimes N}\right]\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[[u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m}]]

(4.42)

Let us calculate the generating function
Z(v)
:

\displaystyle Z(v;u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m})=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}Z_{N}(u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m})v^{N}

(4.43)

using (
4.42
). In doing so we will use the expansion (
4.41
), rewrite the tensor products using (
4.27
) viewing
H_{k}^{\prime(i)}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, and using the fact that
\Psi
is a homomorphism of commuting shuffle algebras:

\displaystyle Z(v;u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m})=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}v^{N}\Psi[\hat{u}^{\otimes N}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}v^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa=(\kappa_{1}\ldots\kappa_{n+m}):\\
|\kappa|=N\end{subarray}}u_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots u_{n+m}^{\kappa_{n+m}}\Psi\left[H_{\kappa_{1}}^{\prime(1)}*^{\prime}\cdots*^{\prime}H_{\kappa_{n}}^{\prime(n)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}v^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa=(\kappa_{1}\ldots\kappa_{n+m}):\\
|\kappa|=N\end{subarray}}u_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots u_{n+m}^{\kappa_{n+m}}\Psi\left[H_{\kappa_{1}}^{\prime(1)}\right]*\cdots*\Psi\left[H_{\kappa_{n}}^{\prime(n)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Psi\left[H^{\prime(1)}(vu_{1})\right]*\cdots*\Psi\left[H^{\prime(n+m)}(vu_{n+m})\right]

(4.44)

With the help of Theorem
4
we obtain the formula:

\displaystyle Z(v;u_{1}\ldots u_{n+m})=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{v^{k}}{k}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+m}\epsilon_{j}^{k}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}k/2}-q^{\delta_{i=j}k}t^{-\epsilon_{j}k/2}}{1-q^{k}}q^{k\delta_{i>j}}P_{k}^{(i)}u_{j}^{k}\right)

(4.45)

The exponent simplifies if we express it in terms of the elements
S_{k}^{(j)}
instead of
P_{k}^{(i)}
, see (
4.37
). After introducing the variables
w_{i}:=t^{-\epsilon_{i}/2}\epsilon_{i}u_{i}
and
w_{n+m+1}:=qw_{1}
we obtain the formula (
4.38
).
∎

4.7.
Computing elements of
\mathcal{B}^{+}
using the linear map
\widetilde{\Psi}

In this section we demonstrate how the linear map
\widetilde{\Psi}
works in specific examples. We will write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m}),\quad\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})

(4.46)

when referring to the commutative subalgebras
\mathcal{B}^{+}\subset\mathcal{A}^{+}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
built out from the
R
-matrix (
2.38
). The maps
\widetilde{\Psi}
will be restricted to the commuting subalgebras and thus we will write:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n^{\prime}|m^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})

(4.47)

In what follows, we will use the notation in Subsection
3.4
.

4.7.1.
Reproducing
\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}_{k}\right]
using
\widetilde{\Psi}

In the first example we set:

\displaystyle V^{\prime\prime}=V\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n+m},\quad V^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{C},\quad\iota=\text{identity map},\quad\iota^{\prime}(1)=j

(4.48)

where we fix
j\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{I}
.
In this case we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})

(4.49)

The role of
H^{\prime(j)}_{k}
is played by the element
H^{\prime}_{k}
which is a
1\times 1
matrix with the matrix element being the symmetric rational function in
(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
which is equal to
1
. Applying
\widetilde{\Psi}
to
H^{\prime}_{k}
and using the definition (
3.28
) gives:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime}_{k}\right]={\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\left(\iota^{\prime\otimes k}H^{\prime}_{k}\pi^{\prime\otimes k}\right)\right)\right]

(4.50)

The expression
\iota^{\prime\otimes k}H^{\prime}_{k}
is equal to
E_{jj}^{\otimes k}
hence we may write:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime}_{k}\right]={\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\check{R}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right)\right]

(4.51)

The right hand side of (
4.51
) coincides with
\Psi\left[H^{\prime(j)}_{k}\right]
. Let
H^{\prime}(x)
be the generating function of
H^{\prime}_{k}
, then by (
4.37
) we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime}(x)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{j}^{k}\left(t^{-\epsilon_{j}k/2}S_{k}^{(j-1)}-t^{\epsilon_{j}k/2}S_{k}^{(j)}\right)\right)

(4.52)

4.7.2.
Computing elements exponentially generated by
S_{k}^{(j)}

In this example we set:

\displaystyle V^{\prime\prime}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n+m+1},\quad V^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{C},\quad\iota(i)=\begin{cases}i&\text{if}\quad i<a\\
i+1&\text{if}\quad i\geqslant a\end{cases},\qquad\iota^{\prime}(1)=a

(4.53)

where we fix
a\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
. The projection
\pi:V^{\prime\prime}\to V
removes the
a
-th coordinate. Recall that
V
has gradation given by
\epsilon_{1}\ldots\epsilon_{n+m}
and let
\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}\in\{\pm 1\}
be the grading of the single vector of
V^{\prime}
. Define the gradation of
V^{\prime\prime}
as:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{i}:=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{i}&\text{if}\quad i<a\\
\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}&\text{if}\quad i=a\\
\epsilon_{i-1}&\text{if}\quad i>a\end{cases},\qquad i=1\ldots n+m+1

(4.54)

The two possible values of
\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}
give us two maps of the form:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})

(4.55)

Proposition 8
.

With the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
as constructed above we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime}(x)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{1}^{\prime k+1}\left(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}\right)S_{k}^{(a-1)}\right)

(4.56)

Proof.

Let us compute
\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H_{k}^{\prime}\right]
using (
3.28
). We note that
\iota^{\prime\otimes k}H_{k}^{\prime}=E_{aa}^{\otimes k}
hence:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H_{k}^{\prime}\right]=\pi^{\otimes k}{\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\right)\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes E_{aa}^{\otimes k}\right)\right]\iota^{\otimes k}

(4.57)

The expression between
\pi^{\otimes k}
and
\iota^{\otimes k}
is of the form (
4.51
) where
j=a
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
is taken as
\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m})
or
\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m+1})
depending on the sign of
\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
. Let
\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m})\subset\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
(or
\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m+1})\subset\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
) denote the counterpart of
S_{k}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})
. With this notation and (
4.52
) we can write the generating function of
\widetilde{\Psi}[H_{k}^{\prime}]
from (
4.57
) as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime}(x)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{1}^{\prime k}\left(t^{-\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}k/2}\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a-1)}\iota^{\otimes k}\right)-t^{\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}k/2}\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a)}\iota^{\otimes k}\right)\right)\right)

(4.58)

Using (
4.19
) we compute:

\displaystyle\pi\widetilde{S}_{1}^{(j)}\iota=\begin{cases}S_{1}^{(j)}&\text{if}\quad j<a\\
S_{1}^{(j-1)}&\text{if}\quad j\geqslant a\end{cases}

(4.59)

From the recursive formula (
4.18
) with
\ell=1
we find:

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(j)}\iota^{\otimes k}=\begin{cases}S_{k}^{(j)}&\text{if}\quad j<a\\
S_{k}^{(j-1)}&\text{if}\quad j\geqslant a\end{cases}

(4.60)

For
j=a-1,a
we have:

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a-1)}\iota^{\otimes k}=S_{k}^{(a-1)},\qquad\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a)}\iota^{\otimes k}=S_{k}^{(a-1)}

(4.61)

After inserting this into (
4.58
) we obtain:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime}(x)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{1}^{\prime k+1}\left(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}\right)S_{k}^{(a-1)}\right)

(4.62)

where we used the identity:

\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2})=(t^{-\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}k/2}-t^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}k/2}),\qquad\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}\in\{\pm 1\}

∎

4.7.3.
Computing the elements
S_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
.

In this example we derive a trace formula for the elements
S_{k}^{(i)}
. The derivation is based on the following. Suppose we have an identity:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}G^{(i)}_{k}(u)v^{k}=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{v^{k}}{k}(u^{k}-1)S^{(i)}_{k}\right)

(4.63)

where
G^{(i)}_{k}(u)
is given by a formula, then we can compute
S^{(i)}_{k}
using:

\displaystyle S^{(i)}_{k}=\frac{d}{du}G^{(i)}_{k}(u)\Big|_{u=1},\qquad k>0

(4.64)

Let us construct a map
\widetilde{\Psi}
which will produce such elements
G^{(i)}_{k}(u)
. We set:

\displaystyle V^{\prime\prime}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n+m+2},\quad V^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad\iota(i)=\begin{cases}i&\text{if}\quad i<a\\
i+2&\text{if}\quad i\geqslant a\end{cases},\qquad\iota^{\prime}(1)=a,\quad\iota^{\prime}(2)=a+1

(4.65)

where we fix
a\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}
. The projection
\pi:V^{\prime\prime}\to V
removes the
a
-th and
a+1
-st coordinates. The gradation of
V^{\prime}
is fixed to be:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}=1,\quad\epsilon^{\prime}_{2}=-1

(4.66)

then the gradation of
V^{\prime\prime}
is:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{i}:=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{i}&\text{if}\quad i<a\\
\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}=1&\text{if}\quad i=a\\
\epsilon^{\prime}_{2}=-1&\text{if}\quad i=a+1\\
\epsilon_{i-1}&\text{if}\quad i>a+1\end{cases}

(4.67)

With this data we have a map
\widetilde{\Psi}
of the form:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1|1})\to\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})

(4.68)

Proposition 9
.

With the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
as constructed above we have:

\displaystyle S_{k}^{(a-1)}=\frac{1}{t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}}\widetilde{\Psi}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{k}(-1)^{l+1}lH_{k-l}^{\prime(1)}*^{\prime}H_{l}^{\prime(2)}\right)

(4.69)

Proof.

Let us apply the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
to
H^{\prime(1)}_{k}
and
H^{\prime(2)}_{k}
. Using (
3.28
) and
\iota^{\prime}
from (
4.65
) we can write:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}_{k}\right]=\pi^{\otimes k}{\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\right)\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes E_{aa}^{\otimes k}\right)\right]\iota^{\otimes k}

(4.70)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(2)}_{k}\right]=\pi^{\otimes k}{\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\right)\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes E_{a+1a+1}^{\otimes k}\right)\right]\iota^{\otimes k}

(4.71)

Next we compute the generating functions of (
4.70
) and (
4.71
) similarly to (
4.58
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{1}^{\prime k}\left(t^{-\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}k/2}\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a-1)}\iota^{\otimes k}\right)-t^{\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}k/2}\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a)}\iota^{\otimes k}\right)\right)\right)

(4.72)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(2)}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\epsilon_{2}^{\prime k}\left(t^{-\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}k/2}\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a)}\iota^{\otimes k}\right)-t^{\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}k/2}\left(\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a+1)}\iota^{\otimes k}\right)\right)\right)

(4.73)

where
\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m+1})\subset\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
denotes the counterpart of
S_{k}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n|m})
. By the same logic leading to (
4.61
) we have:

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a-1)}\iota^{\otimes k}=S_{k}^{(a-1)},\qquad\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a)}\iota^{\otimes k}=S_{k}^{(a-1)},\qquad\pi^{\otimes k}\widetilde{S}_{k}^{(a+1)}\iota^{\otimes k}=S_{k}^{(a-1)}

(4.74)

This gives us the following formulas:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k}\left(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}\right)S_{k}^{(a-1)}\right)

(4.75)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(2)}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}x^{k}}{k}\left(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}\right)S_{k}^{(a-1)}\right)

(4.76)

where we substituted
\epsilon^{\prime}_{1}=1
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{2}=-1
. For our final step we need to show that the map
\widetilde{\Psi}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}_{k}*^{\prime}H^{\prime(2)}_{l}\right]=\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}_{k}\right]*\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(2)}_{l}\right]

(4.77)

Let
V_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})}\subset V^{\prime\prime\otimes(k+l)}
be the coordinate subspace which is spanned by the vectors:

\left|\sigma(a\ldots a,a+1\ldots a+1\right\rangle,\qquad\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}/\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}

where
a
’s appear
k
times and
a+1
’s appear
l
times. Further we define the subspace:

\mathcal{V}_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})}=\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q,t)}\left(V_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})}(z_{1}\ldots z_{k+l})\right)\subset\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}

This space is isomorphic to
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Consider the expression:

\displaystyle E^{\otimes k}_{aa}*^{\prime}E^{\otimes l}_{a+1a+1}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m+1})

(4.78)

where
*^{\prime}
is the shuffle product of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m+1})
. Because of the presence of the projectors
E^{\otimes k}_{aa}
and
E^{\otimes l}_{a+1a+1}
in (
4.78
) this tensor is an element of the subspace
\mathcal{V}_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})}
.
This follows from definitions (
3.4
) and (
3.2
) and the following properties of the
R
-matrix:

(E^{\otimes k}_{aa}\otimes{\text{id}}^{\otimes l})\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{(k+l)}}(E^{\otimes l}_{a+1a+1}\otimes{\text{id}}^{\otimes k})\in\mathcal{V}_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})}

and

X\check{R}_{\sigma},~\check{R}_{\sigma}X\in\mathcal{V}_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})},\qquad\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l},\quad\forall X\in\mathcal{V}_{(a^{k},(a+1)^{l})}

This leads us to the following equation:

\displaystyle\iota^{\prime\otimes(k+l)}\left(E^{\otimes k}_{11}*^{\prime}E^{\otimes l}_{22}\right)\pi^{\prime\otimes(k+l)}=E^{\otimes k}_{aa}*^{\prime}E^{\otimes l}_{a+1a+1}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m+1})

(4.79)

where on the left hand side
*^{\prime}
is the shuffle product of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1|1})
and on the right hand side
*^{\prime}
is the shuffle product of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n+1|m+1})
. Using (
4.79
) we can write the left hand side of (
4.77
) as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}_{k}*^{\prime}H^{\prime(2)}_{l}\right]=\pi^{\otimes N}\left({\text{Tr}}_{N+1\ldots 2N}\left[\left(\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{N}}(qz,z)\right)\left({\text{id}}_{V^{\prime\prime}}^{\otimes N}\otimes\left(H^{\prime(a)}_{k}*^{\prime}H^{\prime(a+1)}_{l}\right)\right)\right]\right)\iota^{\otimes N}

(4.80)

where
N=k+l
. The expression on the right hand side of (
4.80
) respects the shuffle algebras’ multiplication (see Remark
2
). Hence the right hand side of (
4.80
) factorizes and we obtain (
4.77
).

With (
4.75
), (
4.76
) and (
4.77
) we can compute
\widetilde{\Psi}
of a product of two generating functions:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[H^{\prime(1)}(x)H^{\prime(2)}(-ux)\right]=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{-x^{k}}{k}\left(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}\right)\left(u^{k}-1\right)S_{k}^{(a-1)}\right)

(4.81)

This expression is of the form (
4.63
) with
x
playing the role of
v
. Therefore we can use (
4.64
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\left[\sum_{l=0}^{k}(-1)^{l}lH_{k-l}^{\prime(1)}*^{\prime}H_{l}^{\prime(2)}\right]=-\left(t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}\right)S_{k}^{(a-1)}

(4.82)

After a simple rearrangement we get (
4.69
).
∎

Using Remark
3
compute
H_{k-l}^{\prime(1)}*^{\prime}H_{l}^{\prime(2)}
in terms of matrix units. After inserting the result into (
4.69
) and shifting the index
a\rightarrow a+1
we obtain a trace formula for
S_{k}^{(a)}
:

\displaystyle S_{k}^{(a)}=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{t^{-k/2}-t^{k/2}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes k}{\text{Tr}}_{k+1\ldots 2k}\left[\check{R}^{(V^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega^{k}}(qz,z)\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\sum_{\lambda\in\{a+1,a+2\}^{k}}(-1)^{m_{a+2}(\lambda)}m_{a+2}(\lambda)\bigotimes_{i\in\lambda}E_{ii}\right)\right]\iota^{\otimes k}

(4.83)

where the trace is taken over
k
copies of
V^{\prime\prime}
. In the graphical form this equation corresponds to (
1.17
) from the Introduction.

Appendix A
A trace identity from crossing unitarity

In this section we derive an identity related to
R
-matrices of
U_{t}(\dot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
and their crossing unitarity property (
2.9
). This identity involves tensors
F\in{\text{End}}(V^{\otimes l})
and
G\in{\text{End}}(V^{\otimes k})
, for
l,k>0
, which are not necessarily elements of shuffle algebras. The spectral parameters will not change as they pass through these tensors, hence their diagrams look as follows:

(A.1)

Lemma 4
.

Let
k,l
be two positive integers.
Let
z=(z_{1}\ldots z_{k})
,
w=(w_{1}\ldots w_{l})
and
\omega^{j}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}
a
j-
cycle.
For two tensors
F\in{\text{End}}(V^{\otimes l})
and
G\in{\text{End}}(V^{\otimes k})
we have:

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}}\left[F\otimes G\right]

\displaystyle={\text{Tr}}\left[\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{k}}(w,z)F_{1\ldots l}\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)G_{1\ldots k}\right]

(A.2)

with the trace taken over
V^{\otimes(k+l)}
.

Proof.

We provide a recursive proof of this identity starting from the right hand side and showing how the
\check{R}
matrices can be removed with the help of the crossing unitarity. The sequence of algebraic manipulations is guided by the graphical representation.

We note that
\omega^{k}=\omega^{-l}
. Let
\tilde{\omega}^{\pm(l-1)}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l-1}
and
\tilde{w}=(w_{1}\ldots w_{l-1})
denote the counterparts of
\omega^{\pm l}
and the alphabet
w
in which
l
is decreased by
1
. Denote the right hand side of (
A.2
) by
Y

\displaystyle Y={\text{Tr}}\left[\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{k}}(w,z)F_{1\ldots l}\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)G_{1\ldots k}\right]

(A.3)

Graphically we have:

(A.4)

where the first equality represents (
A.3
) and the second equality represents the equation which we need to prove.
Suppose (
A.2
) holds when the tensor
F
is replaced by any tensor in
{\text{End}}(V^{\otimes{(l-1)}})
. Let us show that:

\displaystyle Y={\text{Tr}}\left[\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\tilde{\omega}^{-(l-1)}}(\tilde{w},z)\left({\text{Tr}}_{k+l}F_{1\ldots l-1,k+l}\right)\check{R}_{\tilde{\omega}^{(l-1)}}(z,\tilde{w})G_{1\ldots k}\right]

(A.5)

with the outer trace taken over
V^{\otimes(k+l-1)}
. This will give use a proof by induction.

We can write the two blocks
\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)
and
\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{-l}}(w,z)
in (
A.3
)
as follows:

\displaystyle\check{R}_{\omega^{l}}(z,w)=\check{R}_{l}(w_{l}/z_{1})\cdots\check{R}_{k+l-1}(w_{l}/z_{k})\times\check{R}_{\tilde{\omega}^{l-1}}(z,\tilde{w})

(A.6)

\displaystyle\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\omega^{-l}}(w,z)=\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\tilde{\omega}^{-(l-1)}}(\tilde{w},z)\times\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-1}(z_{k}/w_{l})\cdots\check{R}^{\bullet}_{l}(z_{1}/w_{l})

(A.7)

The individual matrices
\check{R}_{l+i-1}(w_{l}/z_{i-l+1})
and
\check{R}_{k+l-i}^{\bullet}(z_{k-i+1}/w_{l})
,
i=1\ldots k
, in these two expressions correspond to the crossings associated with the “innermost” vector space carrying the parameter
w_{l}
in the middle term in (
A.4
). Insert (
A.6
) and (
A.7
) into (
A.3
):

\displaystyle Y={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-1}(z_{k}/w_{l})X^{(k,l)}\check{R}_{k+l-1}(w_{l}/z_{k})\cdots\right]

(A.8)

where
\cdots
contain tensors acting on the spaces
1\ldots k+l-1
and
X^{(k,l)}
is given by:

\displaystyle X^{(k,l)}=X^{(k,l)}_{1\ldots k+l-1}:=\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-2}(z_{k-1}/w_{l})\cdots\check{R}^{\bullet}_{l}(z_{1}/w_{l})F_{1\ldots l}\check{R}_{l}(w_{l}/z_{1})\cdots\check{R}_{k+l-2}(w_{l}/z_{k-1})

(A.9)

We can replace
X^{(k,l)}_{1\ldots k+l-1}
with
X^{(k,l)}_{1\ldots k+l-2,k+l}
using permutation matrices
P_{k+l-1}
as follows:

\displaystyle Y={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-1}(z_{k}/w_{l})P_{k+l-1}X^{(k,l)}_{1\ldots k+l-2,k+l}P_{k+l-1}\check{R}_{k+l-1}(w_{l}/z_{k})\cdots\right]

(A.10)

Next we apply the transposition
T_{k+l}
inside the trace and compute
7
7

7

In this computation we used the identity
{\text{Tr}}\left[F^{T_{i}}G^{T_{i}}\right]={\text{Tr}}\left[FG\right]
which holds for any two matrices
F,G\in\text{End}(V^{\otimes N})
and
i=1\ldots N
.
:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots\left(\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-1}(z_{k}/w_{l})P_{k+l-1}\right)^{T_{k+l}}\left(X_{1\ldots k+l-2,k+l}P_{k+l-1}\check{R}_{k+l-1}(w_{l}/z_{k})\right)^{T_{k+l}}\cdots\right]

\displaystyle={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots\left(\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-1}(z_{k}/w_{l})P_{k+l-1}\right)^{T_{k+l}}\left(P_{k+l-1}\check{R}_{k+l-1}(w_{l}/z_{k})\right)^{T_{k+l}}\left(X_{1\ldots k+l-2,k+l}\right)^{T_{k+l}}\cdots\right]

\displaystyle={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots\left(X_{1\ldots k+l-2,k+l}\right)^{T_{k+l}}\cdots\right]={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots X_{1\ldots k+l-2,k+l}\cdots\right]

(A.11)

To go from the second line to the third we used the crossing unitarity (
2.9
).
This computation brings
(
A.8
) to the form:

\displaystyle Y={\text{Tr}}\left[\cdots\check{R}^{\bullet}_{k+l-2,k+l}(z_{k-1}/w_{l})X^{(k-1,l)}\check{R}_{k+l-2,k+l}(w_{l}/z_{k-1})\cdots\right]

(A.12)

where we used the explicit form of
X^{(k,l)}
in (
A.9
). This expression for
Y
has the same structure as (
A.8
) therefore we can reuse (
A.10
) and (
A.11
). Performing this computation in total
k
times gives us the following expression for
Y
:

\displaystyle Y={\text{Tr}}\left[\check{R}^{\bullet}_{\tilde{\omega}^{-(l-1)}}(\tilde{w},z)\tilde{F}_{1\ldots l-1}\check{R}_{\tilde{\omega}^{(l-1)}}(z,\tilde{w})G_{1\ldots k}\right]

(A.13)

where
\tilde{F}_{1\ldots l-1}
equals to a partially traced matrix
F
:

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{1\ldots l-1}:={\text{Tr}}_{k+l}X^{(1,l)}_{1\ldots l-1,k+l}={\text{Tr}}_{k+l}F_{1\ldots l-1,k+l}

From the graphical point of view this computation corresponds to pulling the innermost vector spaces and removing all its crosses in the middle term of (
A.4
):

\displaystyle Y=\quad\hbox to83.36pt{\vbox to142.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.03128pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(A.14)

In this computation we derived (
A.5
) which completes the proof.

Appendix B
Computing evaluations
\alpha_{i}

In this Appendix we compute the evaluation
\alpha_{i}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)
. The result is summarized in the following Lemma.

Lemma 5
.

We have the following identity:

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\left(\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]\right)=\begin{cases}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}&i=j\\
q^{k\delta_{i>j}}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l-1}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}&i\neq j\end{cases}

(B.1)

Proof.

According to Definition
9
, in order to compute
\alpha_{i}
, we need to evaluate the residue of
\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]
at:

\left\{z_{1}=y,\ldots,z_{k}=q^{k-1}y\right\}

and express it in the form
(
2.22
), i.e.:

\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y,\ldots,z_{k}=q^{k-1}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{k-1}\prod_{l=1}^{k-2}f\left(q^{l}\right)^{k-1-l}\times\check{R}_{1}\left(q^{-1}\right)\cdots\check{R}_{k-1}\left(q^{-k+1}\right)X_{k}^{(k)}(y)

(B.2)

This will give us an explicit expression for
X_{k}^{(k)}(y)
in which we can take the coefficient of
E_{ii}
and derive (
B.1
).

For clarity we demonstrate the logic of the computation by considering the case
k=3
. The residue of
\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes 3}\right]
is:

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y,z_{2}=qy,z_{3}=q^{2}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes 3}\right]=\quad\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y,z_{2}=qy,z_{3}=q^{2}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}\hbox to123.31pt{\vbox to121.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 23.52281pt\lower-10.8799pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}

(B.3)

The trace can be immediately computed because
E_{jj}
select the
j
-th matrix element in each traced tensor factor.
The poles in the expression on the right hand side in (
B.3
) can only arise in the matrices
\check{R}(z)
when
z\to 1
. We can compute the corresponding residues using (
2.50
) which results into replacing some
\check{R}
matrices with identity matrices and an overall power of
t^{1/2}-t^{-1/2}
. In the computation below we mark these
\check{R}
matrices with circles:

(B.8)

This computation generalizes to the case of
\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]
as follows.

•

The circled
\check{R}
matrices as in (
B.4
) will appear in the row of crosses which is immediately below the middle row. In this row there are
k-1
such matrices. Using (
2.50
) for each one of them will give rise to the factor:

\left(t^{1/2}-t^{-1/2}\right)^{k-1}

•

After the above step we will see
k-2
instances where the unitarity equation (
2.49
) can be applied. This will result in the factor:

f(q)^{k-2}

and in further
k-3
instances where the unitarity equation (
2.49
) can be applied again. This will produce the factor:

f(q^{2})^{k-3}

This pattern will repeat itself. The outcome of this step of the computation produces the net factor:

\prod_{l=1}^{k-2}f\left(q^{l}\right)^{k-1-l}

and an expression analogous to (
B.5
).

•

In order to arrive at the expression (
B.7
) for general
k
we use the same logic to remove
k-1
crosses on the right and introduce the corresponding matrix elements of the
R
-matrix. By collecting the factors which were obtained so far in this computation we obtain:

\displaystyle\underset{\left\{z_{1}=y,\ldots,z_{k}=q^{k-1}y\right\}}{{\text{Res}}}\Psi\left[E_{jj}^{\otimes k}\right]=(t^{1/2}-t^{-1/2})^{k-1}\prod_{l=1}^{k-2}f\left(q^{l}\right)^{k-1-l}\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{-l}t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{-l}}\hbox to60.7pt{\vbox to57.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.67093pt\lower 28.25276pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(B.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t^{1/2}-t^{-1/2})^{k-1}\prod_{l=1}^{k-2}f\left(q^{l}\right)^{k-1-l}\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{-l}t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{-l}}

(B.10)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\check{R}_{1}(q^{-1})\cdots\check{R}_{k-1}(q^{-(k-1)}){\text{Tr}}_{k+1}\left(\check{R}_{k}(q^{-k})\left({\text{id}}^{\otimes k}\otimes E_{jj}\right)\right)

The trace in (
B.10
) can be calculated using the explicit form of
\check{R}
from (
2.38
)-(
2.40
):

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}}_{2}\left(\check{R}(q^{-k})\left({\text{id}}\otimes E_{jj}\right)\right)=\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{-k}t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{-k}}E_{jj}+\frac{\epsilon_{j}(t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}-t^{\epsilon_{j}/2})}{1-q^{-k}}\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n+m}\delta_{i\neq j}q^{-k\delta_{i<j}}E_{ii}

where we used the identity:

t^{-1/2}-t^{1/2}=\epsilon_{j}(t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}-t^{\epsilon_{j}/2})

Comparing the final answer for the residue with (
2.22
) gives us a formula for
X_{k}^{(k)}
:

\displaystyle X^{(k)}=\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}E_{jj}+\prod_{l=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\frac{t^{\epsilon_{j}/2}-q^{l-1}t^{-\epsilon_{j}/2}}{1-q^{l}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant n+m\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}q^{k\delta_{i>j}}E_{ii}

(B.11)

From this we read the coefficient of
E_{ii}
and find (
B.1
).
∎
